### CHILL at SemEval-2025 Task 2: You Can't Just Throw Entities and Hope -- Make Your LLM to Get Them Right
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13070v1)
- **Authors**: Jaebok Lee, Yonghyun Ryu, Seongmin Park, Yoonjung Choi
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we describe our approach for the SemEval 2025 Task 2 on Entity-Aware Machine Translation (EA-MT). Our system aims to improve the accuracy of translating named entities by combining two key approaches: Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) and iterative self-refinement techniques using Large Language Models (LLMs). A distinctive feature of our system is its self-evaluation mechanism, where the LLM assesses its own translations based on two key criteria: the accuracy of entity translations and overall translation quality. We demonstrate how these methods work together and effectively improve entity handling while maintaining high-quality translations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a system for Entity-Aware Machine Translation (EA-MT), focusing on accurate translation of named entities. The approach combines Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) with a self-refinement technique.  It retrieves entity information (labels and descriptions) from Wikidata using the provided Wikidata IDs. It then uses a Large Language Model (LLM) and prompt engineering to generate initial translations. Crucially, the system incorporates a self-evaluation mechanism where the LLM assesses its own translations based on entity label accuracy and overall translation quality, iteratively refining the output.  Experiments on the SemEval 2025 Task 2 dataset demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach, showing improvements over a baseline LLM and a RAG-only approach.  The paper includes case studies demonstrating the benefits of the feedback mechanism.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper addresses a well-defined and important problem: accurate translation of named entities, a task where literal translation often fails.
    *   **Effective Combination of Techniques:** The combination of RAG and self-refinement is a logical and effective way to address the EA-MT problem. RAG provides the necessary knowledge, and self-refinement helps ensure the knowledge is correctly applied.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:**  The results are compelling, showing significant improvements with the RAG + self-refinement approach across various language pairs. The consistent gains demonstrate the robustness of the method.
    *   **Focus on Practicality:** The system relies on prompt engineering with GPT-4, indicating a practical approach that can be readily implemented and doesn't require specialized model training.
    *   **Well-Defined Self-Evaluation:** The inclusion of explicit criteria (entity accuracy and translation quality) provides a structured way for the LLM to refine its output.
    *   **Use of a Realistic Evaluation Setting:** Testing the method in the SemEval Task 2 framework allows for direct comparison with other systems and provides a benchmark for the performance of the method.
    *   **Case Studies:** Provide some insight as to why the model is improving over iterations.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Gold Entity IDs:** A significant limitation is the reliance on gold Wikidata IDs provided by the task organizers. In real-world scenarios, entity identification is a major challenge, and the paper does not address this aspect.  This severely limits the novelty and practical applicability.
    *   **Limited Novelty in Techniques:**  RAG and self-refinement are established techniques.  The novelty lies in *applying* them to the EA-MT task and structuring the prompts/feedback mechanisms. This is not a large contribution, comparatively.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The iterative self-refinement process involves multiple LLM calls, increasing computational cost and latency. The paper mentions budget constraints led to a limit of two iterations, and further exploration of cost-benefit tradeoffs would have been beneficial.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Error Types:** While the paper provides case studies, a more detailed analysis of the types of errors corrected through self-refinement would provide further insights into the system's capabilities and limitations. For example, are improvements more common for entities with more ambiguous labels?
    *   **Lack of Comparison with Other EA-MT methods:** The paper compares with only a baseline GPT-4, which doesn't give an idea of how the method compares to alternatives.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper demonstrates the potential of combining RAG and self-refinement for EA-MT.
    *   The results highlight the importance of incorporating external knowledge and feedback mechanisms for accurate translation of named entities.
    *   The approach is practical and can be readily implemented using existing LLMs.
    *   The paper provides a valuable baseline for future research in EA-MT.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a competent application of known techniques (RAG and self-refinement) to a relevant task (EA-MT). The experimental results are strong, demonstrating clear improvements over a baseline. However, the reliance on gold entity IDs and the relatively limited novelty of the techniques prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. While the work provides a useful demonstration of a practical approach, its impact is somewhat limited by the unaddressed entity identification challenge. Furthermore, the comparison is done only with a baseline GPT-4, which restricts the evaluation of the method. For these reasons, a score of 6 out of 10 is appropriate.

### Discerning What Matters: A Multi-Dimensional Assessment of Moral Competence in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13082v1)
- **Authors**: Daniel Kilov, Caroline Hendy, Secil Yanik Guyot, Aaron J. Snoswell, Seth Lazar
- **Abstract**: Moral competence is the ability to act in accordance with moral principles. As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in situations demanding moral competence, there is increasing interest in evaluating this ability empirically. We review existing literature and identify three significant shortcoming: (i) Over-reliance on prepackaged moral scenarios with explicitly highlighted moral features; (ii) Focus on verdict prediction rather than moral reasoning; and (iii) Inadequate testing of models' (in)ability to recognize when additional information is needed. Grounded in philosophical research on moral skill, we then introduce a novel method for assessing moral competence in LLMs. Our approach moves beyond simple verdict comparisons to evaluate five dimensions of moral competence: identifying morally relevant features, weighting their importance, assigning moral reasons to these features, synthesizing coherent moral judgments, and recognizing information gaps. We conduct two experiments comparing six leading LLMs against non-expert humans and professional philosophers. In our first experiment using ethical vignettes standard to existing work, LLMs generally outperformed non-expert humans across multiple dimensions of moral reasoning. However, our second experiment, featuring novel scenarios designed to test moral sensitivity by embedding relevant features among irrelevant details, revealed a striking reversal: several LLMs performed significantly worse than humans. Our findings suggest that current evaluations may substantially overestimate LLMs' moral reasoning capabilities by eliminating the task of discerning moral relevance from noisy information, which we take to be a prerequisite for genuine moral skill. This work provides a more nuanced framework for assessing AI moral competence and highlights important directions for improving moral competence in advanced AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses a crucial question in the field of AI safety: how do we accurately assess the moral competence of Large Language Models (LLMs)? The authors argue that existing evaluation methods are flawed, primarily because they over-rely on pre-packaged moral scenarios with pre-highlighted moral features. This, they claim, inflates the perceived moral reasoning abilities of LLMs by sidestepping the essential human task of *discerning* moral relevance from irrelevant details.

To overcome these limitations, the paper introduces a novel methodology for evaluating moral competence across five dimensions: identifying morally relevant features, weighting their importance, assigning moral reasons, synthesizing coherent moral judgments, and recognizing information gaps. They conduct two experiments. The first, using standard ethical vignettes, shows LLMs generally outperforming non-expert humans. However, the second experiment, with specifically designed "noisy" scenarios, reveals a striking reversal: LLMs perform *worse* than humans in identifying morally salient features. The paper concludes that current evaluation methods may significantly overestimate LLMs' true moral reasoning capabilities, as the ability to discern relevance is a prerequisite for genuine moral skill.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its critique of existing evaluation methods and its introduction of a new, more nuanced methodology. The idea that LLMs' performance is artificially inflated due to pre-packaged scenarios is insightful. The decomposition of moral competence into five dimensions is a useful framework. The design of the second experiment, specifically to test moral sensitivity in noisy environments, is also a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** The work has significant implications for the field of AI ethics and AI safety. If LLMs are indeed less morally competent than currently believed, it raises serious concerns about their deployment in morally sensitive applications. The paper's findings call for a re-evaluation of current benchmarks and the development of more realistic evaluation methods. This has the potential to influence future research on aligning AI systems with human values.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   **Well-defined methodology:** The five-dimensional framework is well-defined and theoretically grounded.
    *   **Rigorous experimental design:** The two experiments are well-designed to test the paper's hypotheses.
    *   **Empirical evidence:** The results of the experiments provide strong empirical support for the paper's claims.
    *   **Practical implications:** The paper offers concrete recommendations for improving the assessment of AI moral competence.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Potential for subjectivity in evaluation:** While the methodology is more rigorous than existing approaches, some subjectivity may still be involved in evaluating the LLMs' responses, especially regarding moral justifications and reason synthesis. While efforts are made to include Philosopher expert opinion, the limited quantity and lack of statistical outperformance by philosopher feedback suggests potential issues around this approach to benchmarking.
    *   **Limited scope:** The study focuses on text-based vignettes, which may not fully capture the complexity of real-world moral dilemmas. It would be valuable to extend the analysis to more complex, multi-modal scenarios. The paper does not address the problems created by hallucinations when providing irrelevant information.
    *   **Human Reference:** As the authors concede, the human reference group selected here (Prolific users) are not necessarily "good" at moral reasoning. This is a key weakness of the experimental design.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by prompting researchers to develop more realistic and rigorous methods for evaluating AI moral competence. It highlights the importance of focusing on moral sensitivity, rather than simply verdict prediction. It also underscores the need for benchmarks that reflect the complexity of real-world ethical decision-making. The work could inspire the development of new training methods for LLMs that specifically target moral sensitivity.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper provides a valuable critique of the existing literature and proposes a novel and more nuanced methodology. It generates important empirical results and highlights a key area where LLMs fall short of human capabilities. However, weaknesses around subjective evaluation and the limited scope of the experiments bring the value down. Despite these limitations, the work represents a significant step forward in the challenging task of evaluating AI moral competence and deserves recognition for its potential impact on the field.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Detecting Hard-Coded Credentials in Software Repositories via LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13090v1)
- **Authors**: Chidera Biringa, Gokhan Kul
- **Abstract**: Software developers frequently hard-code credentials such as passwords, generic secrets, private keys, and generic tokens in software repositories, even though it is strictly advised against due to the severe threat to the security of the software. These credentials create attack surfaces exploitable by a potential adversary to conduct malicious exploits such as backdoor attacks. Recent detection efforts utilize embedding models to vectorize textual credentials before passing them to classifiers for predictions. However, these models struggle to discriminate between credentials with contextual and complex sequences resulting in high false positive predictions. Context-dependent Pre-trained Language Models (PLMs) or Large Language Models (LLMs) such as Generative Pre-trained Transformers (GPT) tackled this drawback by leveraging the transformer neural architecture capacity for self-attention to capture contextual dependencies between words in input sequences. As a result, GPT has achieved wide success in several natural language understanding endeavors. Hence, we assess LLMs to represent these observations and feed extracted embedding vectors to a deep learning classifier to detect hard-coded credentials. Our model outperforms the current state-of-the-art by 13% in F1 measure on the benchmark dataset. We have made all source code and data publicly available to facilitate the reproduction of all results presented in this paper.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes using Large Language Models (LLMs) like BERT and GPT-2 to detect hard-coded credentials in software repositories. The authors argue that LLMs, due to their contextual understanding capabilities, can overcome limitations of traditional pattern-based and embedding-based methods that struggle with complex and contextualized credentials. They extract embeddings from LLMs and feed them into a deep learning classifier. The paper claims a significant performance improvement (13% F1-measure improvement over the state-of-the-art on a benchmark dataset) compared to existing credential detection tools. The authors evaluated BERT and GPT-2 against various credential detection tools.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses an important problem:** Hard-coded credentials are a persistent security threat.
*   **LLM application:** The use of LLMs is a relevant and timely approach to improving credential detection.  Leveraging LLMs for security tasks is an active research area.
*   **Performance improvement:** The claimed improvement in F1-measure over existing tools is a significant positive. GPT2 significantly outperformed BERT in both intraclass and interclass distance. This suggests GPT2 has better feature extraction capabilities for this particular problem.
*   **Rigorous comparison:** The paper compares the proposed models with a decent set of existing approaches and tools.
*   **Reproducibility:** Publicly available code and data support reproducibility.
*   **Ethical considerations:** The paper addresses ethical considerations related to data privacy and repository owners.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset limitations:** The paper relies primarily on the CredData dataset, which, as acknowledged by the authors, has a modest size.  The performance on this dataset might not translate directly to other real-world scenarios with different distributions of code and credential types. This limits the generalizability of the results. The CredData dataset is also somewhat artificial; it can be argued the dataset has artificial examples of vulnerable code that aren't realistic of production implementations.
*   **Limited LLM scope:** Although the approach uses LLMs, BERT and GPT-2 are relatively older models. While the authors justify this by citing computational efficiency, it begs the question of how much *more* the performance would improve with modern LLMs like LLaMA or Falcon.  The justification is practical but doesn't address the potential ceiling of the proposed technique with more capable models.
*   **Incremental Novelty:** The overall approach, feeding LLM embeddings into a classifier, is somewhat standard. The novelty lies in applying this existing approach to a specific security problem.  The architectural novelty of the *model* itself is low.
*   **Inference time differences:** The latency comparison is biased to GPT2, because GPT2 has 1.871 times lower execution time representation than GPT2. There is a bias to the tool that provides superior performance at detection of hard-coded credentials with minimal performance overhead to developers in CI.

**Significance:**

The paper demonstrates the potential for LLMs to improve credential detection accuracy. If the reported performance gains are robust across different datasets and real-world scenarios, the work could lead to more effective security tools and reduced risk of data breaches. However, the dataset limitations and the use of older LLMs temper the significance.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses, and after critical consideration of the contribution's originality and impact, the paper earns a score of **7**.

*   The problem is important and the application of LLMs is promising. The performance improvement is also a strong point.

*   However, the reliance on a single, somewhat limited dataset and the use of relatively older LLMs are considerable weaknesses that limit the broader impact and generalizability of the findings. The approach itself is fairly incremental, building on existing methods of using embeddings for classification rather than introducing a fundamentally new technique. While the application is novel, the underlying architecture and approach are not groundbreaking.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Rethinking Test-Time Scaling for Medical AI: Model and Task-Aware Strategies for LLMs and VLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13102v1)
- **Authors**: Gyutaek Oh, Seoyeon Kim, Sangjoon Park, Byung-Hoon Kim
- **Abstract**: Test-time scaling has recently emerged as a promising approach for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models or vision-language models during inference. Although a variety of test-time scaling strategies have been proposed, and interest in their application to the medical domain is growing, many critical aspects remain underexplored, including their effectiveness for vision-language models and the identification of optimal strategies for different settings. In this paper, we conduct a comprehensive investigation of test-time scaling in the medical domain. We evaluate its impact on both large language models and vision-language models, considering factors such as model size, inherent model characteristics, and task complexity. Finally, we assess the robustness of these strategies under user-driven factors, such as misleading information embedded in prompts. Our findings offer practical guidelines for the effective use of test-time scaling in medical applications and provide insights into how these strategies can be further refined to meet the reliability and interpretability demands of the medical domain.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Rethinking Test-Time Scaling for Medical AI: Model and Task-Aware Strategies for LLMs and VLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates test-time scaling strategies for Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) in the medical domain. It comprehensively evaluates different approaches to test-time scaling, including varying token budgets, and sequential vs. parallel scaling, across diverse medical tasks and models. The study also examines the robustness of these strategies under user-driven factors, such as misleading information in prompts. The paper provides practical guidelines for effective test-time scaling in medical applications, considering model characteristics and task complexity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Empirical Analysis:** The paper presents a thorough empirical evaluation of various test-time scaling strategies, considering different model architectures (LLMs and VLMs), medical datasets, and scaling techniques (token budget variation, sequential/parallel scaling). This provides a solid foundation for understanding the effectiveness of these strategies.
*   **Model and Task Awareness:** The paper's core contribution lies in emphasizing model- and task-aware test-time scaling. It highlights that the optimal scaling strategy depends on the specific model's characteristics (reasoning ability, output style) and the complexity of the medical task (question answering, calculation). This is a significant insight, moving beyond a one-size-fits-all approach.
*   **Robustness Evaluation:** Assessing the robustness of test-time scaling under user-driven factors, such as misleading information in prompts, is crucial for real-world medical applications. The paper identifies how different scaling strategies respond to such perturbations and the factors that make models more susceptible.
*   **Practical Guidelines:** The study distills its findings into practical guidelines, making the research immediately applicable to practitioners in the medical AI field. This increases the paper's potential impact.
*   **Addressing VLMs:** Previous works mainly focus on test-time scaling for LLMs. This work specifically investigates scaling for VLMs in medical applications, which is essential given the growing relevance of VLMs in clinical and diagnostic contexts.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Techniques:** While the application of test-time scaling strategies to the medical domain is relevant, the techniques themselves (token budget variation, sequential/parallel scaling) are not entirely novel. The paper's novelty primarily lies in the empirical analysis and the model/task awareness.
*   **Focus on Open-Source Models:** The study primarily focuses on open-source models. While this makes the research accessible, it would be valuable to include proprietary models like GPT, Gemini, or Claude for a more comprehensive comparison and broader applicability.
*   **Reliance on a Limited Set of Datasets:** While diverse, the set of medical benchmark datasets used is still limited. More diverse datasets including a larger variety of medical tasks could be included for increased generalizability.

* **Lack of theoretical backing:** The paper does not offer any underlying theories or explanations for why a certain scaling strategy works for a certain model or task. Providing this would add novelty and would increase the impact of the research.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes to the growing body of research on improving the reasoning capabilities of LLMs and VLMs without extensive retraining. Its emphasis on model- and task-aware strategies and its evaluation of robustness make it valuable for the medical AI community. The practical guidelines and insights into different scaling techniques can help researchers and practitioners develop more reliable and effective medical AI systems.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper's strengths lie in its comprehensive empirical analysis, emphasis on model/task awareness, and practical guidelines. Its weaknesses are the limited novelty in the techniques themselves, and its focus on open-source models. While the paper does not introduce groundbreaking new techniques, it provides valuable insights into the effective application of existing techniques in a critical domain. The paper advances the state of the art by identifying key considerations for test-time scaling in medical AI.

Score: 7


- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Leveraging In-Context Learning for Language Model Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13109v1)
- **Authors**: Shivanshu Gupta, Sameer Singh, Ashish Sabharwal, Tushar Khot, Ben Bogin
- **Abstract**: In-context learning (ICL) with dynamically selected demonstrations combines the flexibility of prompting large language models (LLMs) with the ability to leverage training data to improve performance. While ICL has been highly successful for prediction and generation tasks, leveraging it for agentic tasks that require sequential decision making is challenging -- one must think not only about how to annotate long trajectories at scale and how to select demonstrations, but also what constitutes demonstrations, and when and where to show them. To address this, we first propose an algorithm that leverages an LLM with retries along with demonstrations to automatically and efficiently annotate agentic tasks with solution trajectories. We then show that set-selection of trajectories of similar tasks as demonstrations significantly improves performance, reliability, robustness, and efficiency of LLM agents. However, trajectory demonstrations have a large inference cost overhead. We show that this can be mitigated by using small trajectory snippets at every step instead of an additional trajectory. We find that demonstrations obtained from larger models (in the annotation phase) also improve smaller models, and that ICL agents can even rival costlier trained agents. Thus, our results reveal that ICL, with careful use, can be very powerful for agentic tasks as well.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of in-context learning (ICL) with demonstration selection to improve the performance of large language model (LLM) agents in complex sequential decision-making tasks.  The core contributions are: (1) an algorithm for automatically annotating agentic tasks with solution trajectories using an LLM with retries and demonstration selection, enabling the creation of demonstration pools; (2) an exploration of different demonstration granularities (full trajectories, subtask trajectories, and step-level snippets); (3) a comparison of different demonstration selection methods (ranking-based and set-based); and (4) an evaluation on the AppWorld benchmark, showing that carefully selected demonstrations can significantly improve agent performance, reliability, robustness, and efficiency, even rivaling trained agents. The paper explores the trade-offs between demonstration size and inference cost, showing that small trajectory snippets can provide significant performance gains with minimal overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic exploration of demonstration selection for LLM *agents* performing complex sequential tasks, particularly in the AppWorld environment.  While ICL and demonstration selection have been extensively studied in traditional NLP tasks, their application to agentic tasks is less explored, especially considering the nuances of long trajectories and the need for different granularities of demonstrations. The iterative annotation algorithm is also a valuable contribution, as it addresses the scarcity of agentic task solutions for use as demonstrations. The comparison between set-selection and ranking-based selection for these tasks, along with the use of trajectory snippets, adds further novel insights.

*   **Significance:** The findings of this paper are significant for the following reasons:
    *   **Improved Agent Performance:** The paper demonstrates a substantial improvement in LLM agent performance by leveraging demonstration selection.  This suggests that ICL can be a powerful alternative to expensive fine-tuning or reinforcement learning, especially when combined with intelligent selection strategies.
    *   **Increased Reliability and Robustness:** The results show that demonstration selection not only improves average performance but also increases the reliability (across multiple runs) and robustness (across task variations) of LLM agents. This is crucial for real-world deployment.
    *   **Reduced Inference Cost:** By exploring different demonstration granularities, the paper offers practical strategies for reducing the inference cost associated with ICL. The use of trajectory snippets, in particular, provides a way to maintain performance gains with minimal overhead.
    *   **Accessibility:** The paper highlights a path to using larger models (for annotation) to improve smaller models (for deployment), which enables using smaller, more readily available models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough evaluation of different demonstration selection methods and granularities on a challenging benchmark (AppWorld).
    *   **Practical Insights:**  The paper offers practical insights into the design and implementation of ICL for LLM agents, including recommendations for annotation, selection, and placement of demonstrations.
    *   **Well-written and Clear:**  The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology, results, and implications.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Benchmarking:** While AppWorld is a good environment, generalizing the findings to other agentic tasks with different characteristics could be further substantiated with a broader set of benchmarks.
    *   **Reliance on Large LLMs:**  While the study addresses transferring annotations from larger to smaller LLMs, the annotation phase still relies on a relatively powerful LLM.  Exploring techniques to further reduce the annotation cost would enhance the paper's practical value.
    *   **Relatively Simple Annotation Algorithm:** The annotation algorithm, while effective, could be further refined and made more robust. For instance, incorporating more sophisticated methods to handle annotation failures or improve solution diversity could be beneficial.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on LLM agents and ICL. It provides a valuable framework for studying demonstration selection in sequential decision-making tasks and offers practical guidelines for improving agent performance and efficiency. The findings could also lead to the development of more robust and reliable LLM agents for real-world applications.

Overall, this paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of LLM agents and ICL. It presents novel findings, practical insights, and a comprehensive evaluation that advances our understanding of how to effectively leverage demonstrations for sequential decision-making tasks. The paper's focus on complex agentic tasks, exploration of different demonstration granularities, and comparison of selection methods contribute significantly to the literature.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Overcoming Overfitting in Reinforcement Learning via Gaussian Process Diffusion Policy
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13111v1)
- **Authors**: Amornyos Horprasert, Esa Apriaskar, Xingyu Liu, Lanlan Su, Lyudmila S. Mihaylova
- **Abstract**: One of the key challenges that Reinforcement Learning (RL) faces is its limited capability to adapt to a change of data distribution caused by uncertainties. This challenge arises especially in RL systems using deep neural networks as decision makers or policies, which are prone to overfitting after prolonged training on fixed environments. To address this challenge, this paper proposes Gaussian Process Diffusion Policy (GPDP), a new algorithm that integrates diffusion models and Gaussian Process Regression (GPR) to represent the policy. GPR guides diffusion models to generate actions that maximize learned Q-function, resembling the policy improvement in RL. Furthermore, the kernel-based nature of GPR enhances the policy's exploration efficiency under distribution shifts at test time, increasing the chance of discovering new behaviors and mitigating overfitting. Simulation results on the Walker2d benchmark show that our approach outperforms state-of-the-art algorithms under distribution shift condition by achieving around 67.74% to 123.18% improvement in the RL's objective function while maintaining comparable performance under normal conditions.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Gaussian Process Diffusion Policy (GPDP), a novel reinforcement learning (RL) framework aimed at mitigating overfitting and improving robustness to distribution shifts.  GPDP integrates generative diffusion models with Gaussian Process Regression (GPR).  The diffusion model acts as a policy generator, while GPR guides the generated actions towards maximizing a learned Q-function.  The kernel-based nature of GPR provides uncertainty awareness, enabling exploration of new behaviors when encountering unseen states caused by distribution shifts.  The algorithm is evaluated on the Walker2d benchmark, demonstrating superior performance compared to state-of-the-art algorithms under distribution shift conditions, while maintaining comparable performance under normal conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the specific *integration* of GPR with diffusion models within an RL context to address distribution shifts. While diffusion policies and GPR-based policy representations have been explored individually, their combination for explicitly handling uncertainty and overfitting within an *offline* RL setting appears to be a novel contribution.  The use of GPR to *guide* the diffusion model's reverse process, rather than simply using a diffusion model as a policy *imitator*, is also a key differentiator.  However, the individual components (diffusion models, GPR, offline RL) are well-established, so the primary innovation is in the architectural arrangement and the algorithmic adaptations for this specific task.

**Significance:**  The problem of overfitting and distribution shift is a critical bottleneck in applying RL to real-world scenarios.  If GPDP offers a practical and effective solution to these challenges, it could significantly advance the applicability of RL in domains with inherent uncertainty or changing environments. The paper's focus on *offline* RL is also significant, as it addresses the data efficiency limitations that often hinder the deployment of RL in resource-constrained settings.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly identifies the problem of overfitting due to distribution shifts in RL and motivates the need for more robust policies.
*   **Algorithmic Innovation:**  The GPDP framework combines diffusion models and GPR in a novel and potentially effective way, leveraging the strengths of both techniques. The method for reverting to a non-guided diffusion policy during uncertain states is interesting.
*   **Empirical Validation:**  The experimental results on the Walker2d benchmark demonstrate the superiority of GPDP under distribution shift conditions, providing evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The paper quantifies the improvement, which gives credibility to the work.
*   **Uncertainty Quantification:** The paper provides a justification and rationale for using GPR-based approach, since it provides uncertainty quantification, which is one way to address the challenges of distribution shift and the authors connect it well in their derivation.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:**  While the Walker2d benchmark is a standard environment, it represents a relatively simple and controlled scenario.  The performance of GPDP in more complex, high-dimensional, and real-world environments remains an open question.  More diverse benchmark environments or, ideally, real-world experiments, would strengthen the validation.
*   **GPR Scalability:**  The use of exact inference in GPR, with its O(H³) time complexity, raises concerns about the scalability of GPDP to very large datasets. The authors acknowledge this limitation and suggest restricting the training set size, but this could potentially compromise performance.  Exploring approximate inference techniques for GPR could improve scalability.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The hyperparameter optimization in GPR can be challenging. The sensitivity of GPDP's performance to the choice of hyperparameters is not thoroughly investigated.
*   **Justification of Expert Demonstrations:** The use of expert demonstrations in creating the offline dataset D is not strongly justified. It would be interesting to see the performance of GPDP with datasets collected from less optimal policies.
*   **Theoretical Justification**: While the empirical performance is compelling, a more rigorous theoretical analysis of GPDP's convergence properties and generalization bounds would strengthen the paper.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.  While the individual components of GPDP are not entirely novel, the combination and adaptation of these techniques for handling distribution shifts in offline RL is a valuable contribution.  The empirical results provide promising evidence for the effectiveness of the approach, but the limitations regarding scalability, hyperparameter sensitivity, and scope of evaluation prevent a higher score. The framework shows promise and addresses a relevant challenge. Given the novelty of the approach, the paper makes a significant contribution to the state of RL, despite its limitations. It has the potential to influence future research, especially in adapting robust RL methods for deployment.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Designing Deep Learning Frameworks for LLMs:Challenges, Expectations, and Opportunities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13114v1)
- **Authors**: Yanzhou Mu, Rong Wang, Juan Zhai, Chunrong Fang, Xiang Chen, Jiacong Wu, An Guo, Jiawei Shen, Bingzhuo Li, Zhenyu Chen
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) drive significant advancements in real industry applications. LLMs rely on DL frameworks for efficient model construction, distributed execution, and optimized deployment. Their large parameter scale and long execution cycles place extreme demands on DL frameworks in terms of scalability, stability, and efficiency. Therefore, poor usability, limited functionality, and subtle bugs in DL frameworks may hinder development efficiency and cause severe failures or resource waste. However, a fundamental question remains underinvestigated, i.e., What challenges do DL frameworks face in supporting LLMs? To seek an answer, we investigate these challenges through a large-scale analysis of issue reports from three major DL frameworks (MindSpore, PyTorch, TensorFlow) and eight associated LLM toolkits (e.g., Megatron). We construct a taxonomy of LLM-centric bugs, requirements, and user questions and enrich it through interviews with 11 LLM users and eight DL framework developers, uncovering key technical challenges and misalignments between user needs and developer priorities. Our contributions are threefold: (1) we develop a comprehensive taxonomy comprising four question themes (nine sub-themes), four requirement themes (15 sub-themes), and ten bug themes (45 sub-themes); (2) we assess the perceived importance and priority of these challenges based on practitioner insights; and (3) we identify five key findings across the LLM development and propose five actionable recommendations to improve the reliability, usability, and testability of DL frameworks. Our results highlight critical limitations in current DL frameworks and offer concrete guidance for advancing their support for the next generation of LLM construction and applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the challenges DL frameworks face in supporting Large Language Models (LLMs). It analyzes issue reports from major DL frameworks (PyTorch, MindSpore, TensorFlow) and LLM toolkits (e.g., Megatron) to build a taxonomy of LLM-centric bugs, requirements, and user questions.  It also conducts interviews with LLM users and framework developers to understand their experiences, expectations, and priorities. The study identifies key technical challenges, misalignments between user needs and developer priorities, and concludes with actionable recommendations to improve the reliability, usability, and testability of DL frameworks for the next generation of LLM construction and applications. The paper identifies five key findings related to fragile deployment pipelines, incomplete feature support, execution instability, resource inefficiencies, and weak tooling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic and comprehensive approach to analyzing the specific challenges of supporting LLMs within DL frameworks. While existing research addresses bugs in DL frameworks and techniques for LLM optimization, this paper specifically focuses on the *intersection* of these areas. The large-scale analysis of issue reports coupled with expert interviews provides a valuable dataset and empirical insights.

**Significance:** The significance of this work is considerable. As LLMs become increasingly prevalent, understanding and addressing the challenges in the underlying DL frameworks is crucial for ensuring their reliable, efficient, and scalable development and deployment. The taxonomy and the recommendations offer concrete guidance for both framework developers and researchers working on LLM infrastructure. By highlighting the misalignments between user needs and developer priorities, the study can help bridge the gap and drive targeted improvements. The findings regarding the fragile deployment pipelines, execution instability, and resource inefficiencies are particularly impactful.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Methodology:** The combination of automated issue report analysis and expert interviews provides a robust and well-rounded methodology.
*   **Extensive Dataset:** The analysis covers a significant number of issue reports from multiple DL frameworks and LLM toolkits, enhancing the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Actionable Recommendations:** The paper goes beyond identifying problems and offers concrete, actionable recommendations for improving DL framework support for LLMs.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The findings are directly relevant to practitioners working on LLM development and deployment.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Framework Selection:**  While the chosen DL frameworks are popular, the selection could have been more diverse by including more specialized frameworks, if applicable.
*   **Subjectivity in Labeling:** Despite the efforts to minimize subjectivity in issue labeling, some level of interpretation is inevitable. The reported inter-annotator agreement scores help mitigate this but do not eliminate it entirely.
*   **Generalizability of Interview Findings:** While the interviews provide valuable insights, the number of interviewees is relatively small and may not fully represent the diversity of perspectives in the LLM community.
*   **Limited Focus on Security:** While the paper mentions security accidents in the introduction, the security aspects of DL frameworks in supporting LLMs are not thoroughly explored in the main body. This could be an avenue for future research.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by systematically investigating and characterizing the challenges DL frameworks face in supporting LLMs. The findings provide a valuable roadmap for improving the reliability, usability, and efficiency of these frameworks, and the recommendations are practical and actionable. While some limitations exist, the strengths of the paper outweigh its weaknesses.

Score: 8

**Rationale:**

The paper's novelty and significance are high because it tackles a critical problem that's becoming increasingly important as LLMs proliferate. The systematic approach and the actionable recommendations make it a valuable resource for the community. The limitations related to framework selection and subjectivity in labeling are valid concerns but do not diminish the overall impact of the work. The lack of deep discussion on security keeps it from being a 9 or 10, as those would be for landmark, paradigm-shifting works that are very rare.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### PhenoKG: Knowledge Graph-Driven Gene Discovery and Patient Insights from Phenotypes Alone
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13119v1)
- **Authors**: Kamilia Zaripova, Ege Özsoy, Nassir Navab, Azade Farshad
- **Abstract**: Identifying causative genes from patient phenotypes remains a significant challenge in precision medicine, with important implications for the diagnosis and treatment of genetic disorders. We propose a novel graph-based approach for predicting causative genes from patient phenotypes, with or without an available list of candidate genes, by integrating a rare disease knowledge graph (KG). Our model, combining graph neural networks and transformers, achieves substantial improvements over the current state-of-the-art. On the real-world MyGene2 dataset, it attains a mean reciprocal rank (MRR) of 24.64\% and nDCG@100 of 33.64\%, surpassing the best baseline (SHEPHERD) at 19.02\% MRR and 30.54\% nDCG@100. We perform extensive ablation studies to validate the contribution of each model component. Notably, the approach generalizes to cases where only phenotypic data are available, addressing key challenges in clinical decision support when genomic information is incomplete.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper based on the provided OCR text.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PhenoKG, a novel graph-based approach for predicting causative genes from patient phenotypes in rare monogenic diseases.  The method leverages a knowledge graph (PrimeKG) and combines graph neural networks (GNNs) with transformers to learn informative representations of patient-specific subgraphs constructed from phenotypes and candidate genes. PhenoKG can function with or without an initial candidate gene list, addressing a key challenge in clinical settings where genomic information is incomplete. The authors demonstrate that their model outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods, particularly Shepherd, on the MyGene2 dataset.  They also perform ablation studies to validate the contribution of each model component and analyze patient similarity based on embeddings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents a reasonable degree of novelty, although it builds upon existing ideas. Several aspects contribute to its novelty:

*   **Patient-Specific Graph Construction:** The creation of patient-specific subgraphs from the knowledge graph, guided by the patient's phenotypes, is a crucial aspect of the approach. While the idea of using knowledge graphs for rare disease gene prioritization is not entirely new, the specific method of subgraph construction seems tailored and potentially more effective. This allows to capture nuanced interactions between genes, diseases, and phenotypes.

*   **Hybrid Model Architecture:** The combination of GNNs (specifically GATv2) and transformer-based encoders for learning node and graph embeddings is a relatively innovative combination. GNNs are well-suited for capturing local graph structure, while transformers can model long-range dependencies and global context.

*   **Loss Function Design:** The use of a contrastive loss framework with semi-hard negative mining and a patient similarity loss to encourage consistency across batches contributes to the originality of the method.

*   **Addressing Incomplete Data:** One of the key strengths is the model's ability to operate effectively even without a pre-existing candidate gene list, which is a significant practical consideration in many clinical scenarios.

However, it is also crucial to acknowledge that the individual components of the model (GNNs, transformers, contrastive loss) are well-established techniques. The main novelty lies in their specific combination and application within this particular problem domain. Furthermore, the use of pre-trained embeddings from previous work diminishes the originality of some model components.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the diagnosis and treatment of rare genetic disorders. The benefits of the proposed approach are:

*   **Improved Gene Prioritization:** The results on the MyGene2 dataset demonstrate that PhenoKG can achieve superior performance compared to existing methods in prioritizing causative genes, potentially reducing the "diagnostic odyssey" for patients.

*   **Clinical Applicability:** The ability to function without a pre-defined candidate gene list makes PhenoKG more clinically relevant, as it can assist in cases where genomic information is limited or incomplete.

*   **Interpretability (potential):** The patient-specific subgraph construction could, in principle, offer some degree of interpretability, allowing clinicians to understand why the model made a particular prediction. Although the paper does not extensively explore this.

*   **Scalability:** Graph-based approaches are generally scalable for managing and analyzing large amounts of biomedical data, which is important in the context of rare diseases.

However, it is important to temper expectations. While the performance gains are notable, the absolute performance on the MyGene2 dataset, especially the MRR and nDCG scores, indicates that the problem remains challenging. The model's accuracy remains limited for immediate integration into clinical decision-making tools. The study also needs to be expanded upon with prospective validation in real-world clinical settings to confirm the predictions. The experiments are performed on existing datasets, so it’s difficult to completely assess how well it will scale to new genetic conditions.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear problem definition and motivation.
*   Well-defined methodology with detailed explanations of the model architecture and training procedure.
*   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with comparisons to a strong baseline.
*   Ablation studies to validate the contribution of individual model components.
*   Analysis of patient similarity based on embeddings.
*   The implementation uses established machine learning libraries.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The performance on real-world datasets (MyGene2) is not as high as on the simulated dataset, indicating a possible domain adaptation issue.
*   The interpretability of the model is not thoroughly explored.
*   The experimental validation is limited to existing datasets and lacks prospective clinical validation.
*   Relatively high reliance on pre-trained embeddings.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper can influence the field by:

*   Inspiring further research on graph-based methods for rare disease gene prioritization.
*   Promoting the use of hybrid model architectures combining GNNs and transformers in biomedical applications.
*   Highlighting the importance of addressing the challenge of incomplete data in clinical decision support systems.
*   Providing a benchmark for future research in this area.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel and significant contribution to the field of rare disease gene prioritization. It introduces an innovative graph-based approach (PhenoKG) that combines GNNs and transformers, demonstrably outperforming existing methods on a benchmark dataset. A key strength of the model is its potential for clinical applicability, as it can function without pre-existing candidate gene lists.

However, it is also important to note that the individual components of the model are not entirely novel, and the performance gains are not dramatically high, indicating that the problem remains challenging. The lack of thorough interpretability analysis and the limited experimental validation (no prospective clinical validation) also temper the overall assessment. The reliance on pre-trained embeddings also limits the originality.

Therefore, a score of 6.5 reflects the paper's solid contribution with room for improvement and further investigation. The method is novel, but the limited real-world dataset performance and lack of prospective validation mean it does not merit a higher score. The paper's contribution is valuable to this area of research.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### ZINA: Multimodal Fine-grained Hallucination Detection and Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13130v1)
- **Authors**: Yuiga Wada, Kazuki Matsuda, Komei Sugiura, Graham Neubig
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) often generate hallucinations, where the output deviates from the visual content. Given that these hallucinations can take diverse forms, detecting hallucinations at a fine-grained level is essential for comprehensive evaluation and analysis. To this end, we propose a novel task of multimodal fine-grained hallucination detection and editing for MLLMs. Moreover, we propose ZINA, a novel method that identifies hallucinated spans at a fine-grained level, classifies their error types into six categories, and suggests appropriate refinements. To train and evaluate models for this task, we constructed VisionHall, a dataset comprising 6.9k outputs from twelve MLLMs manually annotated by 211 annotators, and 20k synthetic samples generated using a graph-based method that captures dependencies among error types. We demonstrated that ZINA outperformed existing methods, including GPT-4o and LLama-3.2, in both detection and editing tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ZINA, a novel method for multimodal fine-grained hallucination detection and editing in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs).  It proposes a two-stage approach: a detector MLLM (Mdet) identifies hallucinated spans, and a reviewer MLLM (Mrev) classifies error types (using a defined taxonomy) and suggests refinements. The key idea is to decouple token copying (handled deterministically) from the detection/editing tasks, allowing the MLLMs to focus on their respective specialties. The paper also presents VisionHall, a new dataset comprising manually annotated outputs from various MLLMs and synthetically generated samples with error dependencies captured through a graph-based approach.  Experiments demonstrate that ZINA outperforms existing methods, including strong baselines like GPT-4o and Llama-3.2, in both detection and editing, as well as leading to improvement in standard image captioning metrics like CLIP-S and PAC-S.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper offers several novel contributions. Firstly, it tackles the problem of hallucination detection and editing at a *fine-grained* level, which is a significant improvement over existing coarse-grained approaches. The taxonomy of hallucination types (Object, Attribute, Number, Text, Relation, Fact) is well-defined and practical. Secondly, the decoupling strategy (deterministic token copying + MLLM-based detection/editing) is innovative and reduces the complexity of the task for each model.  Thirdly, the graph-based approach for synthetic data generation is a clever way to capture dependencies between different types of errors, addressing a limitation of previous data augmentation techniques. Finally, the VisionHall dataset fills a gap, providing a resource specifically designed for fine-grained hallucination evaluation and editing.
* **Significance:** Hallucinations are a major obstacle to the deployment of MLLMs in real-world applications. By improving the ability to detect and correct these errors at a granular level, this work could contribute to the development of more reliable and trustworthy MLLMs.  The improvement in image captioning metrics (CLIP-S and PAC-S) demonstrates the tangible benefits of the proposed method. The proposed fine-grained detection method also enables future research to analyze error patterns and develop targeted mitigation strategies.  The release of the VisionHall dataset promotes further research in this area by providing a valuable benchmark.

* **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
    *   The decoupling strategy is a smart design choice that simplifies the MLLM's task.
    *   The graph-based synthetic data generation is a significant improvement over previous techniques.
    *   The VisionHall dataset is a valuable contribution to the community.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate a clear improvement over strong baselines.
* **Weaknesses:**
    *   The two-stage architecture may introduce a higher inference time, limiting deployment in resource-constrained environments. While acknowledged, this should be more comprehensively addressed.
    *   Despite showing improvements, the method under-detects hallucinations, suggesting room for improvement in the reviewer MLLM's ability to identify tags.
    *   While the paper shows improvement in image captioning metrics, more direct measures on downstream task performance would strengthen the claims of real-world applicability.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on hallucination detection and editing in MLLMs. The fine-grained approach, decoupling strategy, and graph-based data augmentation are valuable ideas that can be adopted and extended by others. The VisionHall dataset will likely become a standard benchmark for evaluating fine-grained hallucination detection methods.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses a crucial problem in the field of MLLMs with a novel and well-executed approach. The introduced ZINA method demonstrably improves upon existing hallucination detection and correction strategies. While the paper has some limitations, the overall contribution is significant, offering valuable insights and resources for the community. The fine-grained analysis and the approach of considering dependencies between hallucinations is a strong contribution.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Adapting LLMs for Minimal-edit Grammatical Error Correction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13148v1)
- **Authors**: Ryszard Staruch, Filip Graliński, Daniel Dzienisiewicz
- **Abstract**: Decoder-only large language models have shown superior performance in the fluency-edit English Grammatical Error Correction, but their adaptation for minimal-edit English GEC is still underexplored. To improve their effectiveness in the minimal-edit approach, we explore the error rate adaptation topic and propose a novel training schedule method. Our experiments set a new state-of-the-art result for a single-model system on the BEA-test set. We also detokenize the most common English GEC datasets to match the natural way of writing text. During the process, we find that there are errors in them. Our experiments analyze whether training on detokenized datasets impacts the results and measure the impact of the usage of the datasets with corrected erroneous examples. To facilitate reproducibility, we have released the source code used to train our models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the adaptation of large language models (LLMs) for minimal-edit grammatical error correction (GEC). The authors address the problem of LLMs tending to overcorrect, and propose a novel training schedule that controls the precision-recall trade-off during training, aiming for higher precision. They detokenize common GEC datasets and train LLMs, finding that annotation errors exist even in popular datasets. They introduce data augmentation by using corrected text as input and target and find that edited examples improve precision. The method includes a training schedule that separates erroneous and correct examples, fine-tuning the model on correct examples to improve correction precision. The experimental results show that a single Gemma 2 model, trained with their proposed training schedule, achieves a new state-of-the-art result on the BEA-test set. They also show that LLMs perform well in balancing minimal-edit GEC and fluency-edit GEC.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents two key contributions: the training schedule and the error analysis and detokenization of GEC datasets. The error analysis and the dataset detokenization are a useful, albeit incremental, contribution to the field. Identifying and, crucially, making detokenized datasets available is valuable for future research.  The training schedule, focused on controlling the precision-recall tradeoff, is the more significant novel element. Augmenting the data is also an incremental improvement that adds value.
* **Significance:** Achieving a new SOTA result on the BEA-test set is a strong indicator of the paper's significance. The training schedule offers a practical approach to adapting LLMs for minimal-edit GEC, a task where LLMs have previously struggled. Further, identifying and correcting errors in existing datasets has a lasting positive impact on the community, even though it is not the main contribution. The ability to balance minimal edit and fluency edit is significant.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Empirical validation:** The paper thoroughly validates its approach through extensive experiments on standard benchmark datasets.
    * **Clear methodology:** The proposed training schedule and data augmentation techniques are clearly described, making them easy to reproduce.
    * **Practical contribution:** The released detokenized datasets and the proposed training schedule provide valuable resources and guidelines for future research in GEC.
    * **Addresses an important problem:** The paper tackles a specific limitation of LLMs in the context of minimal-edit GEC, which is important for applications requiring precise and accurate corrections.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited model focus:** The primary focus is on the Gemma 2 model, while there is limited generalization to other LLM architectures. However, they did test the Llama 2 architecture.
    * **High sensitivity to hyperparameters:** The training schedule seems to be sensitive to hyperparameter tuning which makes it more challenging to adapt across models.
    * **Incremental improvement:** Although SOTA is achieved, the techniques used are based on well established practices (data augmentation, separate fine-tuning based on data quality).
    * **English language only**: The study is limited to the English Language, and does not address whether these techniques will work on low resource language datasets.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in LLM-based GEC by providing a practical training schedule, corrected datasets, and a deeper understanding of the precision-recall trade-off. The ideas could potentially extend to other tasks where LLMs need to generate strict and controlled outputs, such as code generation. The work is very practical and has influence because other research can immediately benefit from the datasets made available.

**Rationale:**

The paper combines a practical and valuable contribution in the form of the error-corrected datasets with a novel approach to fine-tuning that shows solid empirical results. While the methods aren't revolutionary, they represent a significant improvement on the application of LLMs to the constrained task of minimal-edit GEC and deliver new state-of-the-art performance on a standard benchmark. The weaknesses regarding hyperparameter sensitivity and limited model diversity slightly temper the overall impact.
