### Fair Generation without Unfair Distortions: Debiasing Text-to-Image Generation with Entanglement-Free Attention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13298v1)
- **Authors**: Jeonghoon Park, Juyoung Lee, Chaeyeon Chung, Jaeseong Lee, Jaegul Choo, Jindong Gu
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in diffusion-based text-to-image (T2I) models have enabled the generation of high-quality and photorealistic images from text descriptions. However, they often exhibit societal biases related to gender, race, and socioeconomic status, thereby reinforcing harmful stereotypes and shaping public perception in unintended ways. While existing bias mitigation methods demonstrate effectiveness, they often encounter attribute entanglement, where adjustments to attributes relevant to the bias (i.e., target attributes) unintentionally alter attributes unassociated with the bias (i.e., non-target attributes), causing undesirable distribution shifts. To address this challenge, we introduce Entanglement-Free Attention (EFA), a method that accurately incorporates target attributes (e.g., White, Black, Asian, and Indian) while preserving non-target attributes (e.g., background details) during bias mitigation. At inference time, EFA randomly samples a target attribute with equal probability and adjusts the cross-attention in selected layers to incorporate the sampled attribute, achieving a fair distribution of target attributes. Extensive experiments demonstrate that EFA outperforms existing methods in mitigating bias while preserving non-target attributes, thereby maintaining the output distribution and generation capability of the original model.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Fair Generation without Unfair Distortions: Debiasing Text-to-Image Generation with Entanglement-Free Attention."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of societal biases (related to gender, race, socioeconomic status) present in diffusion-based text-to-image (T2I) models. It identifies a problem called *attribute entanglement*, where existing bias mitigation methods unintentionally alter attributes *unrelated* to the bias when trying to adjust attributes *related* to the bias.  To tackle this, the authors introduce Entanglement-Free Attention (EFA). EFA accurately incorporates target attributes (e.g., specific racial attributes) while preserving non-target attributes (e.g., background details) during bias mitigation.  At inference, EFA randomly samples a target attribute with equal probability and adjusts the cross-attention in selected layers. This achieves a fairer distribution of target attributes in the generated images. Extensive experiments demonstrate that EFA outperforms existing methods in mitigating bias while preserving non-target attributes, thus maintaining the output distribution and generation capability of the original model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The identification of attribute entanglement as a distinct problem in T2I debiasing is novel.  Previous methods often focused on simply reducing bias metrics without carefully considering the impact on the overall distribution or other attributes in the image. The proposed EFA method, with its focus on preserving non-target attributes, is also a novel contribution. Specifically, the training strategy that utilizes counterfactual prompts and human segmentation masks to guide the attention is a key innovation.

*   **Significance:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of responsible AI and generative models. T2I models are increasingly prevalent, and their biases can have real-world consequences. By developing a method that mitigates bias *without* sacrificing generation quality or introducing unintended biases, the authors provide a valuable tool for creating fairer and more reliable T2I systems. The work addresses a crucial limitation of existing bias mitigation approaches.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly articulates the problem of attribute entanglement and its potential negative consequences.
    *   Well-designed method: The EFA method is carefully designed to address the identified problem. The use of counterfactual prompts and segmentation masks is a clever way to train the model to focus on the target attribute while preserving others.
    *   Strong experimental results: The paper presents extensive experimental results that demonstrate the effectiveness of EFA. Quantitative metrics show improvements in bias mitigation and non-target attribute preservation compared to baselines. Qualitative results visually confirm these improvements.
    *   Detailed analysis: The paper includes a detailed analysis of the method, including ablation studies to evaluate the impact of different components and visualizations of attention maps.
    *   The model performs well across different types of bias.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Binary Gender Assumption: While the paper acknowledges that gender is more complex than a binary categorization, the experiments use a binary gender definition due to data and metric limitations. This simplifies a complex societal issue and limits the generalizability of the results.
    *   Limited Scope: The paper primarily focuses on human-centric bias (gender, race, age).  While this is a crucial area, there are other types of biases that can be present in T2I models (e.g., socioeconomic, geographical), and the generalizability of EFA to these other biases is not explored.
    *   Complexity: The method introduces additional complexity to the T2I pipeline.  While the benefits of reduced attribute entanglement are clear, the added computational cost and engineering effort may be a barrier to adoption in some applications.
    *   Dataset limitations: The experiments depend on human segmentation masks, which, while effective, may introduce reliance on datasets where these masks are readily available, potentially limiting the model's use in scenarios where masks are difficult to create.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on debiasing generative models.  The concept of attribute entanglement provides a new framework for understanding and addressing bias, and the EFA method offers a practical solution for mitigating this problem. The methodology (counterfactual prompts and segmentation) could be useful to improve other aspects of image generation beyond just mitigating bias. It could encourage a shift toward more holistic approaches to responsible AI in generative modeling, considering not only bias metrics but also the broader impact on output distributions.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** The paper introduces a novel and significant contribution to the field of debiasing T2I models. The identification and mitigation of attribute entanglement is a crucial step forward. The thorough experimental evaluation and analysis support the effectiveness of the proposed EFA method. However, the limitations related to the binary gender assumption, scope, and complexity prevent it from achieving a higher score. The paper represents a substantial advance but can be seen more as a refined approach to existing methods, rather than a revolutionary change.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Seewo's Submission to MLC-SLM: Lessons learned from Speech Reasoning Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13300v1)
- **Authors**: Bo Li, Chengben Xu, Wufeng Zhang
- **Abstract**: This paper presents Seewo's systems for both tracks of the Multilingual Conversational Speech Language Model Challenge (MLC-SLM), addressing automatic speech recognition (ASR) and speaker diarization with ASR (SD-ASR). We introduce a multi-stage training pipeline that explicitly enhances reasoning and self-correction in speech language models for ASR. Our approach combines curriculum learning for progressive capability acquisition, Chain-of-Thought data augmentation to foster intermediate reflection, and Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) to further refine self-correction through reward-driven optimization. This approach achieves substantial improvements over the official challenge baselines. On the evaluation set, our best system attains a WER/CER of 11.57% for Track 1 and a tcpWER/tcpCER of 17.67% for Track 2. Comprehensive ablation studies demonstrate the effectiveness of each component under challenge constraints.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper describes Seewo's system for the Multilingual Conversational Speech Language Model Challenge (MLC-SLM). The core contribution is a multi-stage fine-tuning pipeline designed to improve the reasoning and self-correction abilities of speech language models for ASR and speaker diarization. The pipeline includes curriculum learning, Chain-of-Thought data augmentation for intermediate reflection, and Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) to refine self-correction. The architecture leverages the Whisper-large-v3-Turbo encoder and the Babel-9B-Chat decoder. The paper presents results demonstrating improvements over the challenge baselines and includes ablation studies to evaluate the effectiveness of each component. The pipeline achieved 11.57% WER/CER for ASR and 17.67% tcpWER/tcpCER for speaker diarization. Experiments also investigated the impact of conversational context and different decoding hyperparameters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a well-structured and clearly described approach to the MLC-SLM challenge. The use of a multi-stage training pipeline incorporating curriculum learning, CoT augmentation, and RLVR is a logical and potentially effective way to improve the reasoning and self-correction capabilities of speech language models. The experiments are comprehensive, including ablation studies to demonstrate the impact of individual components. The result of substantial improvement over the baseline is compelling.

However, the novelty of individual components is limited. The use of CoT and RL for improving reasoning and self-correction is inspired by existing work. The specific implementation details and combination of these techniques, along with curriculum learning and the specific architecture choices, represent the main contribution. A weakness lies in the relatively shallow investigation of the error types and their resolution. The focus is primarily on improving metrics rather than deeply understanding the model's failure modes and how the individual techniques address them. The "reward hacking" observation and the limited success of refining error detail identification suggest that further investigation is needed to improve the sophistication of the reasoning process. The benefit of using ground truth transcriptions for evaluating the impact of conversational context is only an upper bound, and the performance may decrease when using actual generated transcripts.

The choice of Whisper-large-v3-Turbo as the encoder seems sound given its multilingual capabilities and parameter efficiency. Similarly, the Babel-9B-Chat decoder is a reasonable choice considering its training data and strong multilingual performance. The architecture is relatively straightforward.

The significance lies in demonstrating that these existing techniques can be effectively combined and fine-tuned for multilingual conversational speech recognition tasks. The challenge setting (MLC-SLM) and the performance improvements relative to the baseline give credibility to the approach. However, the paper lacks a detailed comparison to other state-of-the-art methods beyond the baseline.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper contributes a well-engineered and effective system for the MLC-SLM challenge. The individual techniques (CoT, RLVR, Curriculum Learning) are not entirely novel, but their combination within a multi-stage training pipeline and their adaptation for the multilingual ASR task, supported by ablation studies, constitute a meaningful contribution. The improvements over the baseline are substantial and validate the effectiveness of the approach.  However, the shallow analysis of error resolution and the lack of comparison to other sophisticated models limit the paper's overall impact and novelty.  The paper is also limited to a challenge dataset, limiting its generalizability.

### AttentionDrag: Exploiting Latent Correlation Knowledge in Pre-trained Diffusion Models for Image Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13301v1)
- **Authors**: Biao Yang, Muqi Huang, Yuhui Zhang, Yun Xiong, Kun Zhou, Xi Chen, Shiyang Zhou, Huishuai Bao, Chuan Li, Feng Shi, Hualei Liu
- **Abstract**: Traditional point-based image editing methods rely on iterative latent optimization or geometric transformations, which are either inefficient in their processing or fail to capture the semantic relationships within the image. These methods often overlook the powerful yet underutilized image editing capabilities inherent in pre-trained diffusion models. In this work, we propose a novel one-step point-based image editing method, named AttentionDrag, which leverages the inherent latent knowledge and feature correlations within pre-trained diffusion models for image editing tasks. This framework enables semantic consistency and high-quality manipulation without the need for extensive re-optimization or retraining. Specifically, we reutilize the latent correlations knowledge learned by the self-attention mechanism in the U-Net module during the DDIM inversion process to automatically identify and adjust relevant image regions, ensuring semantic validity and consistency. Additionally, AttentionDrag adaptively generates masks to guide the editing process, enabling precise and context-aware modifications with friendly interaction. Our results demonstrate a performance that surpasses most state-of-the-art methods with significantly faster speeds, showing a more efficient and semantically coherent solution for point-based image editing tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "AttentionDrag" introduces a novel, one-step, training-free method for point-based image editing using pre-trained diffusion models. It leverages the self-attention mechanism within the U-Net module of diffusion models to exploit latent knowledge and feature correlations for precise and semantically consistent image manipulation. The method consists of three main components: Semantic-based Element Movement, Automatic Mask Generation, and Semantic-based Interpolation. These components work together to ensure that edits respect the semantic structure of the image, generate adaptive masks, and seamlessly fill in any gaps created by the editing process. The results demonstrate superior performance compared to existing state-of-the-art methods in terms of both editing quality and computational efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper proposes a novel approach by directly utilizing the self-attention mechanism in pre-trained diffusion models for point-based image editing. This is a clever way to leverage inherent knowledge within the model, avoiding computationally expensive re-optimization or retraining. The approach of using self-attention for both mask generation and semantic interpolation is also innovative.
    *   **Performance:** The experimental results (both qualitative and quantitative) demonstrate the superior performance of AttentionDrag compared to existing methods. It achieves better semantic consistency and editing quality while also being computationally efficient.
    *   **Efficiency:** A key advantage of AttentionDrag is its one-step, training-free nature.  This significantly improves editing speed compared to iterative optimization-based methods, making it more practical for real-time or interactive applications.
    *   **Completeness:** The ablation study thoroughly examines the contribution of each component (mask generation and semantic interpolation) and the influence of key parameters, strengthening the validity of the proposed method.
    *   **Inpainting capability:** The proposed technique can be applied to inpainting.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Experiments:** While the experiments are comprehensive, the dataset used is mainly a collection of well-aligned images, which is less comprehensive. Performance on more challenging datasets with intricate scenes and occlusions could strengthen the claim.
    *   **Dependence on DDIM inversion:** The approach hinges on DDIM inversion. While DDIM is widely used, improvements in inversion techniques could further enhance the results. Discussion of how advances in inversion might impact or be incorporated into AttentionDrag would be beneficial.
    *   **Lack of theoretical proof:** Although the paper shows empirically that AttentionDrag works well, there needs to be a theoretical basis for the process of element movement based on semantic relatedness.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the field of image editing by providing a fast and effective method for point-based manipulation.
    *   The utilization of latent feature correlations within pre-trained diffusion models can inspire future research in other image editing tasks.
    *   The method's computational efficiency makes it attractive for practical applications, potentially enabling real-time or interactive image editing tools.

*   **Justification for Score:**

AttentionDrag exhibits significant novelty in its approach to point-based image editing by directly leveraging the self-attention mechanisms inherent in pre-trained diffusion models. The method's capacity to create adaptive masks and perform semantic interpolation demonstrates a sophisticated understanding of latent space relationships. The performance benefits, in terms of both speed and quality, are significant, establishing it as a valuable contribution to the field. However, there is room for improvement, particularly in establishing the framework's generalizability through the validation of its effectiveness on diverse and more complex datasets and establishing a theoretical foundation.

### Quantitative Comparison of Fine-Tuning Techniques for Pretrained Latent Diffusion Models in the Generation of Unseen SAR Image Concepts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13307v1)
- **Authors**: Solène Debuysère, Nicolas Trouvé, Nathan Letheule, Olivier Lévêque, Elise Colin
- **Abstract**: This work investigates the adaptation of large pre-trained latent diffusion models to a radically new imaging domain: Synthetic Aperture Radar (SAR). While these generative models, originally trained on natural images, demonstrate impressive capabilities in text-to-image synthesis, they are not natively adapted to represent SAR data, which involves different physics, statistical distributions, and visual characteristics. Using a sizeable SAR dataset (on the order of 100,000 to 1 million images), we address the fundamental question of fine-tuning such models for this unseen modality. We explore and compare multiple fine-tuning strategies, including full model fine-tuning and parameter-efficient approaches like Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), focusing separately on the UNet diffusion backbone and the text encoder components. To evaluate generative quality, we combine several metrics: statistical distance from real SAR distributions, textural similarity via GLCM descriptors, and semantic alignment assessed with a CLIP model fine-tuned on SAR data. Our results show that a hybrid tuning strategy yields the best performance: full fine-tuning of the UNet is better at capturing low-level SAR-specific patterns, while LoRA-based partial tuning of the text encoder, combined with embedding learning of the <SAR> token, suffices to preserve prompt alignment. This work provides a methodical strategy for adapting foundation models to unconventional imaging modalities beyond natural image domains.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper investigates fine-tuning pre-trained latent diffusion models (LDMs) for synthetic aperture radar (SAR) image generation.  The authors address the challenge of adapting models trained on natural images to the fundamentally different SAR domain, characterized by unique physics, statistical distributions, and visual features. Using a large SAR dataset, they compare various fine-tuning strategies, including full fine-tuning and parameter-efficient methods like LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation), applied selectively to the UNet backbone and text encoders. The paper introduces a new evaluation framework combining statistical distribution comparisons (KL divergence), texture analysis (GLCM), and semantic alignment (SAR-specific CLIP model). The results suggest a hybrid approach is most effective: full UNet fine-tuning to capture SAR-specific patterns and LoRA on the text encoder along with a learned <SAR> token to preserve prompt alignment. The paper demonstrates the potential for adapting foundation models to unconventional imaging modalities beyond natural images. The paper further explores the impact of noise offsets during training, LoRA rank and alpha parameters, batch size, and VAE decoder fine-tuning to optimise generative capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper addresses an important and relatively unexplored problem: adapting large pre-trained models to the SAR domain. While fine-tuning LDMs is a common practice, applying it to such a drastically different modality like SAR and providing a comparative analysis of different fine-tuning strategies is indeed novel. The proposed evaluation framework is also a significant contribution, as existing image quality metrics are ill-suited for SAR data. While the *individual* components of the proposed strategy may not be radically novel, the holistic approach, encompassing the selection of fine-tuning methods, their architecture-specific application, the inclusion of learned embeddings, and the comprehensive evaluation methodology makes for a substantial innovative leap. The study highlights the importance of domain-specific adaptation, moving beyond simple transfer learning. The analysis of LoRA rank and alpha, noise offset, and the VAE decoder offers a deeper understanding of the fine-tuning process.  Prior work either focuses on training models from scratch on SAR data or relies on simpler transfer learning techniques. This paper tackles the problem head-on, exploring advanced fine-tuning strategies that are more practical than training a model from scratch. The analysis is clearly well-thought-out and has yielded some interesting findings that are new to the area.

*   **Significance:** The work has significant potential impact. SAR imagery is crucial for remote sensing, and generating realistic SAR data can be invaluable for algorithm development, scenario simulation, and data augmentation. Adapting foundation models offers a promising path to overcome data scarcity and improve the performance of SAR-based applications. By providing a practical, methodical strategy for adapting foundation models to non-optical modalities, the paper contributes to the broader AI research and development community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Thorough and well-structured comparative analysis of different fine-tuning strategies.
    *   Introduction of a comprehensive evaluation framework tailored for SAR image generation.
    *   Clear presentation of experimental results and insightful discussions.
    *   Good use of a relatively large SAR dataset.
    *   The study is reproducible and well-documented in terms of methodology and hyperparameter settings.
    *   The paper clearly articulates its findings, limitations, and avenues for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the proposed evaluation framework is a good start, it could benefit from further refinement. For example, subjective human evaluation or comparison against human interpretable features in SAR could be added.
    *   The dataset size, while significant, is still smaller than the datasets used for pre-training the original foundation models, which may limit the achievable performance.
    *   The paper focuses solely on one LDM architecture (SDXL), which may limit the generalizability of the findings to other types of LDMs or other generative models.
    *   Discussion could be expanded to include comparisons to sim-to-real transfer or domain adaptation techniques more frequently found in the remote sensing literature.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to stimulate further research in adapting foundation models for remote sensing and other unconventional modalities. The insights into fine-tuning strategies and evaluation metrics will be valuable to other researchers working in this area.  The hybrid approach, combining full fine-tuning and LoRA, could become a standard practice. The methods presented could further enable downstream tasks such as SAR image segmentation, classification, or change detection by providing more accurate and robust generative data.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing the significant challenge of adapting LDMs to the SAR domain. It introduces a solid methodology, includes a comprehensive evaluation framework, and derives practically valuable insights. There is an adequate amount of novelty within the contribution, which is likely to be followed and built upon by similar studies. However, there is limited generalization across alternative model architectures, datasets, and the lack of a strong comparative study with the existing literature (particularly domain adaption approaches from within remote sensing). Further, refinement of evaluation metrics and techniques would strengthen future studies of a similar nature. Overall, the paper represents a significant, albeit not revolutionary, advance in its area.
