### Large Language Models as 'Hidden Persuaders': Fake Product Reviews are Indistinguishable to Humans and Machines
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13313v1)
- **Authors**: Weiyao Meng, John Harvey, James Goulding, Chris James Carter, Evgeniya Lukinova, Andrew Smith, Paul Frobisher, Mina Forrest, Georgiana Nica-Avram
- **Abstract**: Reading and evaluating product reviews is central to how most people decide what to buy and consume online. However, the recent emergence of Large Language Models and Generative Artificial Intelligence now means writing fraudulent or fake reviews is potentially easier than ever. Through three studies we demonstrate that (1) humans are no longer able to distinguish between real and fake product reviews generated by machines, averaging only 50.8% accuracy overall - essentially the same that would be expected by chance alone; (2) that LLMs are likewise unable to distinguish between fake and real reviews and perform equivalently bad or even worse than humans; and (3) that humans and LLMs pursue different strategies for evaluating authenticity which lead to equivalently bad accuracy, but different precision, recall and F1 scores - indicating they perform worse at different aspects of judgment. The results reveal that review systems everywhere are now susceptible to mechanised fraud if they do not depend on trustworthy purchase verification to guarantee the authenticity of reviewers. Furthermore, the results provide insight into the consumer psychology of how humans judge authenticity, demonstrating there is an inherent 'scepticism bias' towards positive reviews and a special vulnerability to misjudge the authenticity of fake negative reviews. Additionally, results provide a first insight into the 'machine psychology' of judging fake reviews, revealing that the strategies LLMs take to evaluate authenticity radically differ from humans, in ways that are equally wrong in terms of accuracy, but different in their misjudgments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether humans and Large Language Models (LLMs) can distinguish between real and AI-generated product reviews. Through three studies, the authors demonstrate that both humans and LLMs perform only slightly better than chance at identifying fake reviews.  They find that humans are susceptible to a "scepticism bias," being suspicious of overly positive reviews, while LLMs are more influenced by surface-level textual features like review length.  The paper highlights the growing challenge of detecting AI-generated content in consumer-facing environments and discusses the implications for trust and decision-making.  It argues that current AI systems are better at generating convincing text than detecting it, creating a potential "dark side" for AI in consumer contexts.  The paper concludes by suggesting potential solutions like digital identity verification and watermarking, emphasizing the need for ethical and regulatory frameworks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Timely and Relevant:** The paper addresses a highly relevant and increasingly important issue. The proliferation of AI-generated content poses a real threat to the integrity of online reviews and consumer trust.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper employs a well-structured methodology, including human subject experiments and benchmarking against state-of-the-art LLMs. The studies are clearly described, and the data analysis is sound.
    *   **Novel Findings:** The discovery that both humans and LLMs struggle to differentiate between real and fake reviews, and the identification of distinct biases (scepticism bias vs. reliance on surface features), are novel and insightful.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers practical implications for online marketplaces, consumers, and AI developers. The suggestions for digital identity verification and watermarking are valuable.
    *   **Well-Written and Clear:** The paper is well-written, clearly structured, and easy to understand, even for readers who are not experts in the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses on a single product category ("Home and Kitchen" on Amazon) and a specific set of LLMs. While this allows for a controlled experiment, it limits the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Simulated Environment:**  The human study involved participants judging reviews in an artificial setting (i.e., "Imagine you are working in Amazon's Quality Assurance Department").  This may not accurately reflect how consumers evaluate reviews in real-world shopping scenarios.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** The quality and effectiveness of the AI-generated reviews depend heavily on the prompts used. While the authors provide details about their prompt design, there is always a possibility that different prompts could lead to different results.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Countermeasures:** While the paper mentions digital identity verification and watermarking, it does not delve into the practical challenges and limitations of these solutions. A deeper discussion of potential countermeasures would have strengthened the paper.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    * The work's novelty stems primarily from its rigorous empirical assessment of how both humans and machines perform on the same task of detecting AI-generated reviews, revealing that both perform poorly and that they rely on different, flawed heuristics.  This comparative approach and the identification of distinct biases add significant value to the existing literature.

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by providing empirical evidence of a growing problem and offering actionable insights. The rigorous methodology and novel findings warrant a high score. However, the limitations regarding scope and the simulated environment slightly reduce its overall impact.

**Score: 7.5**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Pervasive Distributed Agentic Generative AI -- A State of The Art
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13324v1)
- **Authors**: Gianni Molinari, Fabio Ciravegna
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of intelligent agents and Large Language Models (LLMs) is reshaping the pervasive computing field. Their ability to perceive, reason, and act through natural language understanding enables autonomous problem-solving in complex pervasive environments, including the management of heterogeneous sensors, devices, and data. This survey outlines the architectural components of LLM agents (profiling, memory, planning, and action) and examines their deployment and evaluation across various scenarios. Than it reviews computational and infrastructural advancements (cloud to edge) in pervasive computing and how AI is moving in this field. It highlights state-of-the-art agent deployment strategies and applications, including local and distributed execution on resource-constrained devices. This survey identifies key challenges of these agents in pervasive computing such as architectural, energetic and privacy limitations. It finally proposes what we called "Agent as a Tool", a conceptual framework for pervasive agentic AI, emphasizing context awareness, modularity, security, efficiency and effectiveness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper provides a survey of research on LLM-based agents in pervasive computing environments. It covers the architecture of LLM agents (profiling, memory, planning, and action), their deployment strategies (local vs. distributed across cloud, fog, and edge), alignment strategies (Supervised Fine-Tuning, Direct Preference Optimization, Federated Learning), and how these agents can be adapted (Memory, Reasoning, Planning, and Action) and evaluated for use in constrained settings. It also discusses applications in areas like smart homes, mobile task automation, smart health and factories, and smart cities. The paper identifies key challenges like resource limitations, generalization, memory, energetic, and privacy issues. The paper concludes with a proposed "Agent as a Tool" paradigm where specialized agents are used like individual software tools called upon to act within different pervasive computing environments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper offers a broad overview of LLM agents, covering various architectural components, deployment options, evaluation metrics, and application areas related to pervasive computing.
    *   **"Agent as a Tool" Paradigm:** The "Agent as a Tool" concept is a useful framing for thinking about the future of AI in pervasive environments, suggesting a modular and composable approach.
    *   **Practical Considerations:** The paper highlights the key practical challenges involved in deploying LLM agents in pervasive computing scenarios, such as resource limitations, privacy concerns, and model generalization.
    *   **Well Organized:** The structure of the paper is logical, progressing from general agent architecture to deployment specifics and finally to a future outlook.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Originality:** As a survey, the primary contribution is synthesis and organization rather than novel research findings. It synthesizes existing work rather than introducing new theoretical insights or empirical results, though the "Agent as a Tool" framework, while conceptually simple, is a useful contribution.
    *   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** While the paper mentions various applications and benchmarks, it lacks empirical validation or comparison of different agent architectures or deployment strategies in real-world pervasive computing settings.
    *   **Limited Technical Depth:** While the paper broadly addresses the challenges, it could benefit from a more technical deep-dive into specific architectural solutions or optimization techniques.
    *   **Uneven Coverage:**  Some areas are covered with more depth than others, for example the alignment strategies.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper's primary value lies in synthesizing a rapidly evolving field and highlighting key research directions. The "Agent as a Tool" paradigm presents a valuable framework for conceptualizing future research avenues in pervasive agentic AI. While it does not introduce dramatically new findings, it provides a useful overview of the landscape.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper earns a 7 due to its thorough coverage of the field, identification of key challenges, and the introduction of a conceptual framework in "Agent as a Tool." The survey will likely be helpful for researchers entering the field or looking for a broader understanding of the current state of pervasive agentic AI. Its limitations lie primarily in its lack of original research and a focus more on broad summarization rather than deeply technical analyses or empirical results. While it identifies the key challenges, it doesn't propose specific technical solutions beyond suggesting the importance of research in those areas.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### VIS-Shepherd: Constructing Critic for LLM-based Data Visualization Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13326v1)
- **Authors**: Bo Pan, Yixiao Fu, Ke Wang, Junyu Lu, Lunke Pan, Ziyang Qian, Yuhan Chen, Guoliang Wang, Yitao Zhou, Li Zheng, Yinghao Tang, Zhen Wen, Yuchen Wu, Junhua Lu, Biao Zhu, Minfeng Zhu, Bo Zhang, Wei Chen
- **Abstract**: Data visualization generation using Large Language Models (LLMs) has shown promising results but often produces suboptimal visualizations that require human intervention for improvement. In this work, we introduce VIS-Shepherd, a specialized Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM)-based critic to evaluate and provide feedback for LLM-generated data visualizations. At the core of our approach is a framework to construct a high-quality visualization critique dataset, where we collect human-created visualization instances, synthesize corresponding LLM-generated instances, and construct high-quality critiques. We conduct both model-based automatic evaluation and human preference studies to evaluate the effectiveness of our approach. Our experiments show that even small (7B parameters) open-source MLLM models achieve substantial performance gains by leveraging our high-quality visualization critique dataset, reaching levels comparable to much larger open-source or even proprietary models. Our work demonstrates significant potential for MLLM-based automated visualization critique and indicates promising directions for enhancing LLM-based data visualization generation. Our project page: https://github.com/bopan3/VIS-Shepherd.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper "VIS-Shepherd: Constructing Critic for LLM-based Data Visualization Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VIS-Shepherd, a multimodal large language model (MLLM)-based critic designed to evaluate and provide feedback on data visualizations generated by other LLMs. The core contribution is a framework for constructing a high-quality dataset of visualization critiques. This framework involves curating real-world visualization examples, synthesizing corresponding instructions and datasets, generating LLM-based visualizations (often flawed), and collecting expert annotations providing critiques of those flaws. The paper fine-tunes a 7B-parameter open-source MLLM using this dataset and demonstrates, through automated and human evaluation, that the resulting VIS-Shepherd achieves substantial improvements in critique quality, rivaling much larger models (both open-source and proprietary). The authors showcase that using specialized, high-quality training data is more important than simply model scale for this task.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper demonstrates good novelty in several aspects:

*   **Specialized MLLM Critic for Visualization:** While general and domain-specific critics exist, the focus on *visualizations* and the explicit incorporation of the *visual* input into the critique process is a key differentiator. This addresses the limitation of text-only critiques that are insufficient for visual outputs.
*   **Data-Centric Approach:** The emphasis on a carefully constructed, high-quality critique dataset is significant. The framework for generating this dataset – including the steps of instruction synthesis and flawed visualization generation – offers a valuable methodology for the community. This data-centric perspective moves beyond simply relying on model scale, a common trend in LLM research.
*   **Comprehensive Critique Dataset Construction:** The described dataset curation process is rigorous and systematic. The collection of real-world examples, followed by controlled introduction of flaws and expert annotations, creates a valuable resource not readily available before this work.

**Significance:**

*   **Improved LLM-Based Visualization Generation:**  The direct application of VIS-Shepherd is to enhance the quality of visualizations generated by LLMs. This can lower the barrier to data visualization creation, making it more accessible to a wider audience. By providing automated feedback, suboptimal visualizations can be corrected, reducing the need for human intervention.
*   **Data Quality over Model Scale:** The paper's finding that a smaller model trained on high-quality data can outperform significantly larger models is a strong and important message. This has broader implications for the LLM field, suggesting that focusing on data curation and domain expertise is crucial for creating effective specialized models.
*   **Framework for Creating Visualization Critics:** The methodology presented for constructing the critique dataset provides a valuable template for future research in this area.  The staged process of data gathering, instruction synthesis, and LLM flaw generation, paired with expert annotation, can be adapted to other visualization domains and tasks.
*   **Open Source MLLM Models** The open source nature of the models makes the work more accessible to a wider group of researchers, lowering barriers to entry.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Scope of Visualizations:** While the paper emphasizes diversity, the precise scope and limitations of the visualization types covered could be clearer.  A more detailed analysis of the types of visualization defects VIS-Shepherd can effectively identify would strengthen the evaluation.
*   **Annotation Scope and Bias:** The selection criteria and training of annotators could introduce bias. While the paper mentions checks for consistency, a more in-depth discussion of potential biases and steps taken to mitigate them would improve the trustworthiness of the results.
*   **Generalizability to Other LLM Architectures:**  The paper focuses on fine-tuning Qwen-2.5-VL-7B. While the results are promising, it is unclear how well the approach would generalize to other MLLM architectures. Testing with different base models would broaden the impact.
*   **Limited Evaluation of Real-World Impact:** Evaluation metrics primarily focus on automated model comparison and human preferences. A case study of how VIS-Shepherd is integrated into a LLM-based visualization generation pipeline to improve usability in a real-world scenario would provide additional evidence of the practical significance.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper demonstrates good novelty and significant potential, it's not a groundbreaking paradigm shift. The core contribution is a well-executed data-centric approach for a specific task. The limitations outlined above also slightly constrain the immediate impact. Given the data-centric nature of this work, and the limitations outlined in the weaknesses, the assigned score is justified because there are many similar techniques in LLM-based critic tasks. However, by applying these techniques in a visual, multimodal space, the paper opens new avenues for data-focused LLM critic development.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Document-Level Tabular Numerical Cross-Checking: A Coarse-to-Fine Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13328v1)
- **Authors**: Chaoxu Pang, Yixuan Cao, Ganbin Zhou, Hongwei Li, Ping Luo
- **Abstract**: Numerical consistency across tables in disclosure documents is critical for ensuring accuracy, maintaining credibility, and avoiding reputational and economic risks. Automated tabular numerical cross-checking presents two significant challenges: (C1) managing the combinatorial explosion of candidate instances at the document level and (C2) comprehending multi-faceted numerical semantics. Previous research typically depends on heuristic-based filtering or simplified context extraction, often struggling to balance performance and efficiency. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable contextual understanding capabilities that helps address C2 at the instance level, yet they remain hampered by computational inefficiency (C1) and limited domain expertise. This paper introduces CoFiTCheck, a novel LLM-based coarse-to-fine framework that addresses these challenges through two sequential stages: embedding-based filtering and discriminative classification. The embedding-based filtering stage introduces an instructional parallel encoding method to efficiently represent all numerical mentions in a table with LLMs, as well as a decoupled InfoNCE objective to mitigate the isolated mention problem. The discriminative classification stage employs a specialized LLM for fine-grained analysis of the remaining candidate pairs. This stage is further enhanced by our crosstable numerical alignment pretraining paradigm, which leverages weak supervision from cross-table numerical equality relationships to enrich task-specific priors without requiring manual annotation. Comprehensive evaluation across three types of real-world disclosure documents demonstrates that CoFiTCheck significantly outperforms previous methods while maintaining practical efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CoFiTCheck, a novel coarse-to-fine framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) for document-level tabular numerical cross-checking. It aims to identify and verify semantically equivalent numerical mentions across tables within disclosure documents, a task that is important for ensuring data integrity and preventing reputational/financial risks. CoFiTCheck addresses the scalability challenges of this task through two sequential stages: 1) Embedding-based Filtering (using Contextualized Instructional Parallel Encoding and a decoupled InfoNCE objective) to efficiently reduce the candidate space and 2) Discriminative Classification (using a specialized LLM with Cross-table Numerical Alignment Pretraining (CNAP)) for fine-grained semantic matching. The framework is evaluated on real-world disclosure documents and demonstrates significant improvements over existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers several notable innovations. The coarse-to-fine approach itself, while not entirely new in other domains, is well-motivated in the context of document-level tabular cross-checking. The key novelties lie in:

    *   The Contextualized Instructional Parallel Encoding (CIPE) strategy for efficient LLM-based encoding of multiple numerical mentions within a table. This addresses a significant computational bottleneck.
    *   The decoupled InfoNCE objective, specifically designed to handle the prevalence of isolated mentions in this task. This is a crucial point that improves the performance of the filtering stage.
    *   The Cross-table Numerical Alignment Pretraining (CNAP) paradigm. Using weak supervision from numerical equality relations to pretrain the classifier LLM enhances performance without manual annotation, making it practically useful.
    *   The comprehensive experimental evaluation across different types of real-world financial documents, focusing on real-world data.

*   **Significance:** The task of document-level tabular numerical cross-checking is practically significant, especially in domains like finance, government, and scientific reporting. The paper addresses a real-world need and offers a potentially deployable solution. The gains in performance (approximately 10 points on F1-score) over the existing AutoCheck system are meaningful. The efficiency improvements (significantly faster processing times) are also important for real-world deployment. The CNAP pretraining method is significant because it helps to equip the model with financial domain knowledge, which is critical to achieving reliable performance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed architecture and technical components.
    *   Strong experimental results, demonstrating significant improvements in both accuracy and efficiency.
    *   Ablation studies that provide insights into the contributions of different components.
    *   The CNAP approach is notable because it overcomes the limitations of general purpose LLMs with additional fine-tuning leveraging numerical relationships.
    *   Real world data set used as part of the evaluation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the paper discusses limitations of previous research, a more thorough comparison to general-purpose LLMs in zero/few-shot settings (beyond the experiments in Table III) might be beneficial. How much of the gain comes from domain adaptation vs. specific architectural choices?
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of error cases. What types of errors does the system still make, and why? Are there specific types of tables or numerical mentions that are particularly challenging?
    *   The reliance on a relatively large (7B) LLM in the best-performing configuration could be a barrier to adoption for some users. More analysis of the trade-off between model size and performance would be useful.
    *   The number of baseline comparisons could be more extensive.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in several areas:

    *   Tabular fact checking and verification.
    *   Applications of LLMs to structured data.
    *   Weakly supervised pretraining methods.
    *   Document intelligence and automated auditing.
    *   Demonstrates the potential for LLMs to assist with audit processes and document analysis in specialized real world scenarios.

**Overall:**

This is a strong paper that makes a significant contribution to the field. While there are some limitations, the proposed framework is well-designed, thoroughly evaluated, and addresses a real-world need. The paper introduces several novel techniques that are likely to be of interest to researchers working on tabular data processing and LLMs. The results demonstrate that this method provides a good balance of accuracy and efficiency.
The study also incorporates ablation studies that provide critical analysis of each method and results of this analysis.

**Score: 8** The paper demonstrates novelty and practical value. However, further exploration of failure cases and comparison with fine-tuned general-purpose LLMs, as well as an analysis of the effect of model size on the performance, would significantly enhance the paper's impact and justify a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### NTU Speechlab LLM-Based Multilingual ASR System for Interspeech MLC-SLM Challenge 2025
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13339v1)
- **Authors**: Yizhou Peng, Bin Wang, Yi-Wen Chao, Ziyang Ma, Haoyang Zhang, Hexin Liu, Xie Chen, Eng Siong Chng
- **Abstract**: This report details the NTU Speechlab system developed for the Interspeech 2025 Multilingual Conversational Speech and Language Model (MLC-SLM) Challenge (Task I), where we achieved 5th place. We present comprehensive analyses of our multilingual automatic speech recognition system, highlighting key advancements in model architecture, data selection, and training strategies. In particular, language-specific prompts and model averaging techniques were instrumental in boosting system performance across diverse languages. Compared to the initial baseline system, our final model reduced the average Mix Error Rate from 20.2% to 10.6%, representing an absolute improvement of 9.6% (a relative improvement of 48%) on the evaluation set. Our results demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach and offer practical insights for future Speech Large Language Models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper describes the NTU Speechlab's system for the Interspeech 2025 Multilingual Conversational Speech and Language Model (MLC-SLM) Challenge (Task I).  The system utilizes a Whisper encoder, a lightweight MLP adapter, and a Gemma-2-2B Large Language Model (LLM) backbone. The key contributions highlighted are: 1) full-parameter tuning of the LLM with a frozen Whisper encoder, 2) language-specific prompts for improved multilingual ASR, and 3) model averaging for performance gains.  The system achieved 5th place in the challenge, demonstrating a significant reduction in Mix Error Rate (MER) compared to the baseline. The paper details the architecture, training strategy, and experimental results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a competent application of existing techniques in the field of Speech LLMs for multilingual ASR. The approach of using a pre-trained Whisper encoder and fine-tuning an LLM (Gemma-2-2B) is not fundamentally novel, as many previous works have explored similar architectures, (e.g., Qwen-Audio, SpeechGPT). However, the paper demonstrates the effectiveness of this approach in the context of the MLC-SLM challenge.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough experimental evaluation, including a detailed breakdown of performance across different languages and accents.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The paper offers practical insights into the impact of different training strategies, such as language-specific prompts and model averaging. The use of language-specific prompts is a good idea and is a contribution worth noting.
    *   **Good Results:** The paper shows a significant improvement over the baseline system in terms of MER. The 48% relative improvement is impressive.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty:** The overall architecture and training strategies are largely based on existing work. While demonstrating the efficacy of these techniques is valuable, the incremental novelty is limited. The choice of Gemma-2-2B is a reasonable one, but it is not groundbreaking.
    *   **Incremental improvements:** The individual components only offer minor improvements to the baseline.
    *   **Lack of analysis:** The paper provides limited in-depth analysis of *why* certain techniques work or fail to work for specific languages or accents. For example, it would be interesting to see analysis of the hallucinations observed during inference.
    *   **Modality Adaptor:** While the modality adaptor is mentioned, there isn't any investigation into whether more complex or adaptive approaches to bridging modalities could further improve performance.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper provides a practical guide for building multilingual ASR systems using Speech LLMs.
    *   The results demonstrate the importance of language-specific prompts and model averaging for improving performance in multilingual ASR.
    *   The paper contributes to the growing body of literature on Speech LLMs.

**Overall:** The paper presents a solid engineering effort with some practical insights. The overall approach lacks significant novelty, and a deeper analysis of the results could have strengthened the paper.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**
A score of 6 reflects a contribution that is technically sound and offers some useful practical insights, but lacks significant conceptual novelty or a deeper analytical component. The results are good and the improvements are impressive, but the overall approach builds heavily on existing techniques. Further, the paper's limited discussion of failure cases (hallucinations) and absence of novel theoretical underpinnings restrict its transformative influence within the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### LapDDPM: A Conditional Graph Diffusion Model for scRNA-seq Generation with Spectral Adversarial Perturbations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13344v1)
- **Authors**: Lorenzo Bini, Stephane Marchand-Maillet
- **Abstract**: Generating high-fidelity and biologically plausible synthetic single-cell RNA sequencing (scRNA-seq) data, especially with conditional control, is challenging due to its high dimensionality, sparsity, and complex biological variations. Existing generative models often struggle to capture these unique characteristics and ensure robustness to structural noise in cellular networks. We introduce LapDDPM, a novel conditional Graph Diffusion Probabilistic Model for robust and high-fidelity scRNA-seq generation. LapDDPM uniquely integrates graph-based representations with a score-based diffusion model, enhanced by a novel spectral adversarial perturbation mechanism on graph edge weights. Our contributions are threefold: we leverage Laplacian Positional Encodings (LPEs) to enrich the latent space with crucial cellular relationship information; we develop a conditional score-based diffusion model for effective learning and generation from complex scRNA-seq distributions; and we employ a unique spectral adversarial training scheme on graph edge weights, boosting robustness against structural variations. Extensive experiments on diverse scRNA-seq datasets demonstrate LapDDPM's superior performance, achieving high fidelity and generating biologically-plausible, cell-type-specific samples. LapDDPM sets a new benchmark for conditional scRNA-seq data generation, offering a robust tool for various downstream biological applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LapDDPM, a novel conditional graph diffusion model designed for generating synthetic scRNA-seq data. It aims to address the challenges of high dimensionality, sparsity, and complex biological variations inherent in scRNA-seq data. LapDDPM integrates graph-based representations (k-NN graphs enhanced with Laplacian Positional Encodings) with a score-based diffusion model. A key innovation is a spectral adversarial perturbation mechanism applied to the graph's edge weights during training, designed to enhance robustness against structural noise in cellular networks.  Experiments on various scRNA-seq datasets demonstrate LapDDPM's superior performance in terms of distribution matching (RBF-kernel MMD and 2-Wasserstein distance) and the generation of biologically plausible, cell-type-specific samples.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper demonstrates reasonable novelty through the combination of existing techniques in a new context and by introducing a unique spectral adversarial perturbation method. The use of graph-based representations and Laplacian Positional Encodings for scRNA-seq data isn't entirely new, as GNNs have been applied in this domain. However, the specific integration with a conditional diffusion model and the spectral adversarial perturbation does represent a novel contribution. The spectral perturbation, specifically targeting the principal eigenvector, aims for targeted robustness, which is a clever idea.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential for generating high-quality synthetic scRNA-seq data. Such data could be invaluable for data augmentation (especially for rare cell types), benchmarking computational tools, and performing in-silico perturbation studies. The robustness to structural noise is particularly important, as real biological networks are often incomplete or noisy. The conditional generation aspect is also crucial for practical applications where specific cell-type profiles are needed.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive approach:** The paper combines several powerful techniques (GNNs, diffusion models, adversarial training) in a well-reasoned way.
    *   **Novel spectral adversarial perturbation:** This is the most significant contribution, offering a potentially effective method for improving robustness.
    *   **Thorough evaluation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results across diverse datasets, using established evaluation metrics. Ablation studies provide further insight into the contribution of each component.
    *   **Addresses a relevant problem:** The generation of synthetic scRNA-seq data is a valuable goal with many potential applications.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental advance:** While the components are combined in a novel manner, the individual building blocks (GNNs, diffusion models) are well-established. The advancement is primarily in the *integration* and *application* of these methods.
    *   **Complexity:** The model is relatively complex, involving several components and hyperparameters. This could make it challenging to implement and tune for new datasets.
    *   **Limited ablation study analysis:** While ablations are presented, the paper could benefit from a deeper analysis of *why* removing specific components leads to performance degradation. More detailed discussion on the impact of parameter choices (e.g., in the adversarial training) would be useful.
    *   **Comparison to other diffusion based method:** There are other diffusion-based methods that could have been used for comparison.
    *   **Lack of qualitative results:** Showing some generated examples of gene expression patterns could add insights

*   **Potential Influence:**  If LapDDPM proves to be robust and generalizable across a wide range of scRNA-seq datasets, it could become a valuable tool for researchers in the field. The spectral adversarial perturbation technique could also inspire new approaches for improving the robustness of GNNs in other applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel integration of existing techniques and introduces a unique adversarial training approach that appears promising for generating synthetic scRNA-seq data. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating superior performance compared to existing methods. However, the reliance on established building blocks and the potential complexity of the model limit the overall impact. Therefore, a score of 7 is appropriate. The paper presents a solid contribution, but not a revolutionary breakthrough. Future work demonstrating broader applicability and providing deeper insights into the behavior of the spectral adversarial perturbation would further strengthen the contribution.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Direct Reasoning Optimization: LLMs Can Reward And Refine Their Own Reasoning for Open-Ended Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13351v1)
- **Authors**: Yifei Xu, Tusher Chakraborty, Srinagesh Sharma, Leonardo Nunes, Emre Kıcıman, Songwu Lu, Ranveer Chandra
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have showcased impressive reasoning abilities in structured tasks like mathematics and programming, largely driven by Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), which uses outcome-based signals that are scalable, effective, and robust against reward hacking. However, applying similar techniques to open-ended long-form reasoning tasks remains challenging due to the absence of generic, verifiable reward signals. To address this, we propose Direct Reasoning Optimization (DRO), a reinforcement learning framework for fine-tuning LLMs on open-ended, particularly long-form, reasoning tasks, guided by a new reward signal: the Reasoning Reflection Reward (R3). At its core, R3 selectively identifies and emphasizes key tokens in the reference outcome that reflect the influence of the model's preceding chain-of-thought reasoning, thereby capturing the consistency between reasoning and reference outcome at a fine-grained level. Crucially, R3 is computed internally using the same model being optimized, enabling a fully self-contained training setup. Additionally, we introduce a dynamic data filtering strategy based on R3 for open-ended reasoning tasks, reducing cost while improving downstream performance. We evaluate DRO on two diverse datasets -- ParaRev, a long-form paragraph revision task, and FinQA, a math-oriented QA benchmark -- and show that it consistently outperforms strong baselines while remaining broadly applicable across both open-ended and structured domains.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Direct Reasoning Optimization (DRO), a novel reinforcement learning framework designed to fine-tune Large Language Models (LLMs) for open-ended reasoning tasks. It addresses the challenge of applying RL to tasks lacking generic, verifiable reward signals by proposing the Reasoning Reflection Reward (R3). R3 selectively emphasizes key tokens in the reference outcome that reflect the influence of the model's preceding chain-of-thought reasoning, effectively capturing consistency between reasoning and outcome. DRO leverages R3 in a self-contained training setup, dynamically filtering data to reduce costs and improve performance. The framework is evaluated on ParaRev (paragraph revision) and FinQA (math-oriented QA), showing consistent outperformance against strong baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a relevant and challenging problem in the field of LLMs: how to effectively apply reinforcement learning to open-ended reasoning tasks. RL with verifiable rewards has proven successful in structured domains, but its extension to tasks like text revision or open-ended question answering has been hindered by the difficulty of defining appropriate reward functions.

**Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the R3 reward signal, which aims to capture the quality of reasoning by analyzing the model's certainty about the reference outcome given the generated chain-of-thought. Using the model's own self-certainty to guide training is an interesting approach that avoids reliance on external reward models or hand-crafted metrics. The dynamic data filtering technique based on R3 further enhances the framework's efficiency.

**Significance:** The significance stems from the demonstrated ability of DRO to improve LLM performance on open-ended reasoning tasks, which are crucial for many real-world applications. The results on ParaRev, in particular, highlight DRO's potential to handle tasks involving relatively long-form textual outputs, surpassing even a much larger model like GPT-4. The ability to achieve comparable results on FinQA to methods using verifiable rewards demonstrates the versatility of the framework.

**Strengths:**

*   The R3 reward signal is a well-motivated and innovative approach to address the reward function challenge in open-ended reasoning tasks.
*   The self-contained training setup of DRO, using the same model for reward calculation, eliminates the need for external verifiers and reduces the risk of reward hacking.
*   The dynamic data filtering strategy improves training efficiency and downstream performance.
*   The experimental results on ParaRev and FinQA provide strong evidence of DRO's effectiveness and versatility.
*   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and its underlying motivations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The effectiveness of R3 relies on the quality and relevance of the reference outcomes. If the reference outcome is flawed or doesn't accurately reflect the desired behavior, DRO's performance may be limited.
*   The implementation of R3 involves some design choices (e.g., the weighting function w△(σj), propagation factor Pprop(j)) that may require careful tuning and could be sensitive to different tasks and datasets.
*   While the paper demonstrates DRO's effectiveness on two datasets, further evaluation on a wider range of open-ended reasoning tasks would strengthen the claims of generality.
*   The paper may benefit from more analysis of the types of reasoning errors that DRO is able to correct, and the limitations of the approach in handling certain types of reasoning challenges.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of LLM training by presenting a novel and effective approach to reinforcement learning for open-ended reasoning tasks. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses, and the potential impact on real-world applications is significant.

Score: 8.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### StoryBench: A Dynamic Benchmark for Evaluating Long-Term Memory with Multi Turns
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13356v1)
- **Authors**: Luanbo Wan, Weizhi Ma
- **Abstract**: Long-term memory (LTM) is essential for large language models (LLMs) to achieve autonomous intelligence in complex, evolving environments. Despite increasing efforts in memory-augmented and retrieval-based architectures, there remains a lack of standardized benchmarks to systematically evaluate LLMs' long-term memory abilities. Existing benchmarks still face challenges in evaluating knowledge retention and dynamic sequential reasoning, and in their own flexibility, all of which limit their effectiveness in assessing models' LTM capabilities. To address these gaps, we propose a novel benchmark framework based on interactive fiction games, featuring dynamically branching storylines with complex reasoning structures. These structures simulate real-world scenarios by requiring LLMs to navigate hierarchical decision trees, where each choice triggers cascading dependencies across multi-turn interactions. Our benchmark emphasizes two distinct settings to test reasoning complexity: one with immediate feedback upon incorrect decisions, and the other requiring models to independently trace back and revise earlier choices after failure. As part of this benchmark, we also construct a new dataset designed to test LLMs' LTM within narrative-driven environments. We further validate the effectiveness of our approach through detailed experiments. Experimental results demonstrate the benchmark's ability to robustly and reliably assess LTM in LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces StoryBench, a novel benchmark framework for evaluating long-term memory (LTM) capabilities in large language models (LLMs). StoryBench utilizes interactive fiction games with dynamically branching storylines and complex reasoning structures to simulate real-world scenarios requiring LLMs to navigate hierarchical decision trees. The benchmark features two distinct settings: immediate feedback (where incorrect decisions receive immediate feedback) and self-recovery (where models must independently trace back and revise earlier choices after failure). The authors also construct a new dataset based on the interactive fiction game "The Invisible Guardian" and conduct experiments with several advanced LLMs to validate the effectiveness of the benchmark.  The experiments highlight that while some LLMs demonstrate strong knowledge retention and sequential reasoning, all struggle with self-recovery. The authors also discuss failure cases and limitations of the benchmark.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM evaluation, particularly concerning long-term memory and sequential reasoning. The novelty lies in the benchmark's design, which leverages interactive fiction to create a dynamic and realistic testing environment. This approach addresses some of the limitations of existing benchmarks that often rely on static tasks or simplistic synthetic data.  The "self-recovery" mode is particularly interesting as it forces the model to reason about its past decisions and correct errors without external guidance, which is a crucial aspect of true LTM.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Benchmark Design:** The use of interactive fiction provides a more engaging and realistic simulation of long-term memory tasks than many existing benchmarks. The dynamic and branching narratives offer a richer environment for evaluating sequential reasoning.
*   **Self-Recovery Task:** The introduction of the self-recovery mode is a significant strength. It pushes models beyond simple fact retrieval and requires them to actively reason about their past actions and correct errors, thereby testing higher-level cognitive abilities.
*   **Dataset Construction:** The creation of a custom dataset specifically designed for the benchmark ensures that the evaluation is tailored to the intended objectives, mitigating concerns about data contamination or lack of appropriate complexity.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a thorough evaluation of several advanced LLMs using various metrics, providing a detailed analysis of their strengths and weaknesses.
*   **Clear Failure Analysis:**  The identification and discussion of common failure cases provide valuable insights into the limitations of current LLMs and directions for future research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Domain Specificity:** The use of interactive fiction, while innovative, might limit the generalizability of the benchmark to other domains. While the underlying memory and reasoning challenges are likely to be domain-agnostic to some extent, further work is needed to explore how well the benchmark reflects LTM in other contexts.
*   **Limited Scope of Dataset:**  The current dataset only covers the first five chapters of the interactive fiction game. This restricts the possible storyline length and the complexity of dependencies that can be tested. While significant, it could be extended.
*   **Intervention Metric in Self-Recovery Mode:** While the implementation of an intervention mechanism is designed to avoid models stalling the task, introducing this element may affect the precision of results.
*   **Cost & Accessibility**: There appears to be a reliance on closed-source models, creating some barriers to reproducibility/access for other research groups.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a critical gap in the evaluation of LLMs by providing a more realistic and challenging benchmark for long-term memory. The insights gained from StoryBench can guide the development of more robust and capable LLMs that can effectively handle complex, dynamic, and sequential tasks.  The focus on self-recovery is particularly relevant for applications requiring autonomous decision-making and problem-solving.  The benchmark's design could inspire the development of similar evaluation frameworks in other domains.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is well-written, clearly presents its methodology, and provides compelling experimental results. The novelty of the benchmark design and the focus on self-recovery are particularly valuable. However, the domain specificity and dataset limitations slightly reduce its potential impact.  Also, some issues regarding models used exist. Overall, the paper presents a significant contribution to the field and has the potential to influence future research on LLMs.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Socratic RL: A Novel Framework for Efficient Knowledge Acquisition through Iterative Reflection and Viewpoint Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13358v1)
- **Authors**: Xiangfan Wu
- **Abstract**: Current Reinforcement Learning (RL) methodologies for Large Language Models (LLMs) often rely on simplistic, outcome-based reward signals (e.g., final answer correctness), which limits the depth of learning from each interaction. This paper introduces Socratic Reinforcement Learning (Socratic-RL), a novel, process-oriented framework designed to address this limitation. Socratic-RL operates on the principle that deeper understanding is achieved by reflecting on the causal reasons for errors and successes within the reasoning process itself. The framework employs a decoupled "Teacher-Student" architecture, where a "Teacher AI" analyzes interaction histories, extracts causal insights, and formulates them into structured "viewpoints." These viewpoints, acting as distilled guidance, are then used by a "Student AI" to enhance its subsequent reasoning. A key innovation is the iterative self-improvement of the Teacher AI, enabling its reflective capabilities to evolve through a meta-learning loop. To manage the accumulation of knowledge, a distillation mechanism compresses learned viewpoints into the Student's parameters. By focusing on process rather than just outcome, Socratic-RL presents a pathway toward enhanced sample efficiency, superior interpretability, and a more scalable architecture for self-improving AI systems. This paper details the foundational concepts, formal mechanisms, synergies, challenges, and a concrete research roadmap for this proposed framework.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "Socratic RL: A Novel Framework for Efficient Knowledge Acquisition through Iterative Reflection and Viewpoint Distillation."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Socratic Reinforcement Learning (Socratic-RL), a novel framework for training large language models (LLMs) that moves beyond simple outcome-based reward signals. It proposes a "Teacher-Student" architecture where a "Teacher AI" analyzes the Student AI's reasoning process, extracts causal insights, and formulates them as structured "viewpoints" to guide the Student's future reasoning. A key innovation is a meta-learning loop that allows the Teacher AI to iteratively improve its reflective capabilities. Knowledge Distillation compresses the learned viewpoints into the Student's parameters. The goal is to enhance sample efficiency, interpretability, and scalability for self-improving AI systems by focusing on the reasoning *process* rather than just the final outcome.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core idea of using a "Teacher AI" to provide structured feedback on the reasoning process is novel. While the concepts of knowledge distillation, meta-learning, and AI feedback are established, the specific combination and architecture in Socratic-RL represent a unique approach.  The iterative self-improvement of the Teacher AI, driven by the Student's performance, is a potentially significant contribution, addressing a key limitation of existing AI feedback systems.  The explicit focus on generating *viewpoints* as structured, human-readable guidance contributes to interpretability. It tries to leverage interpretable data to accelerate deep learning.

**Significance:**

*   **Potential Benefits:** The potential benefits of Socratic-RL are significant, especially in domains requiring complex reasoning. Enhanced sample efficiency would reduce training costs. Superior interpretability could improve trust and debuggability. A scalable architecture for self-improvement could accelerate the development of more advanced AI systems.

*   **Challenges and Limitations:** The paper acknowledges several challenges, and these are critical to assessing the significance:

    *   **Subjectivity in Evaluation:** As noted in the paper, the assessment of reasoning is prone to be subjective, and it is quite task-dependent. It is not clear how this problem will be tackled.
    *   **Stability of Self-Improvement:** As acknowledged, there is a high risk of model collapse where the diversity of outputs vanishes over time, as well as bias amplification.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Extra inference passes introduce significant latency.
    *   **Teacher Model Drift:** The "madness" phenomenon can be a potential problem.

*   **Open Questions:** There are a lot of open questions and require further research. How will the viewpoints be selected and structured to maximize their impact? How will the meta-learning loop for the Teacher AI be optimized to ensure stable and effective improvement? How will the knowledge distillation process be designed to preserve the essential information from the viewpoints?
*   **Implementation Details:** The paper lacks concrete implementation details, such as a description of what meta-learning process is used for the teacher. In addition, the paper remains at a high level, there are very little experiments being conducted.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the paper's novelty and potential significance balanced against the acknowledged challenges and lack of concrete implementation details, a score of 6 is appropriate.

*   **Strengths:** Novel architecture, focus on process-oriented learning, iterative self-improvement of the Teacher AI, potential for enhanced sample efficiency and interpretability.

*   **Weaknesses:** High-level conceptual framework with limited implementation details, significant challenges related to subjectivity in evaluation, stability of self-improvement, computational cost, and safety of the teacher model. There are no experiments to prove its effectiveness.

*   **Potential Influence:** Socratic-RL could inspire new approaches to training LLMs that focus on improving the reasoning process. It could also contribute to the development of more interpretable and self-improving AI systems. However, realizing this potential will require addressing the outlined challenges and demonstrating the effectiveness of the framework through comprehensive empirical evaluation.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Decompositional Reasoning for Graph Retrieval with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13380v1)
- **Authors**: Valentin Six, Evan Dufraisse, Gaël de Chalendar
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) excel at many NLP tasks, but struggle with multi-hop reasoning and factual consistency, limiting their effectiveness on knowledge-intensive tasks like complex question answering (QA). Linking Knowledge Graphs (KG) and LLMs has shown promising results, but LLMs generally lack the ability to reason efficiently over graph-structured information. To tackle this problem, we propose a novel retrieval approach that integrates textual knowledge graphs into the LLM reasoning process via query decomposition. Our method decomposes complex questions into sub-questions, retrieves relevant textual subgraphs, and composes a question-specific knowledge graph to guide answer generation. For that, we use a weighted similarity function that focuses on both the complex question and the generated subquestions to extract a relevant subgraph, which allows efficient and precise retrieval for complex questions and improves the performance of LLMs on multi-hop QA tasks. This structured reasoning pipeline enhances factual grounding and interpretability while leveraging the generative strengths of LLMs. We evaluate our method on standard multi-hop QA benchmarks and show that it achieves comparable or superior performance to competitive existing methods, using smaller models and fewer LLM calls.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach for knowledge-guided reasoning with Large Language Models (LLMs) by using query decomposition and graph retrieval.  The method decomposes complex questions into a series of logically ordered sub-questions, retrieves relevant textual subgraphs for each sub-question, and then merges these subgraphs to form a question-specific knowledge graph. This graph is used to guide the LLM in generating more accurate and interpretable answers. The approach uses a hybrid retrieval mechanism that combines the original complex question and its sub-questions to extract a more relevant subgraph.  Experiments on CWQ and WebQSP datasets demonstrate improvements in accuracy and factual consistency compared to existing methods, while also reducing the number of LLM calls.  The paper also explores the impact of various parameters, such as the weighting between the original question and subquestions during retrieval, and the size of the extracted subgraphs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the integration of query decomposition with graph retrieval in a way that is specifically designed to improve LLM reasoning over structured knowledge. While knowledge graph-augmented LLMs and query decomposition techniques exist, the combination of these techniques, along with a hybrid retrieval method, presents a unique contribution. The idea of iterative, context-aware retrieval conditioned on previous sub-question answers also adds a layer of sophistication. The hybrid retrieval method balances global and local context which addresses a common problem.

* **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in addressing the limitations of LLMs in multi-hop reasoning, factual grounding, and the efficient use of structured knowledge.  By providing a structured reasoning pipeline, the method improves the factual correctness and interpretability of LLM predictions.  The experimental results demonstrate that the approach can achieve comparable or superior performance to existing methods using smaller models and fewer LLM calls, thus improving efficiency. The reduced LLM calls are significant from both a cost and environmental perspective. The experiments are performed on well-established datasets.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined method:** The paper clearly outlines the steps involved in the proposed approach, including query decomposition, subgraph retrieval, and answer generation.
    * **Hybrid Retrieval:** The use of a hybrid similarity function to guide the retrieval process is a valuable contribution. Balancing the influence of both the complex question and its decomposition addresses a key retrieval challenge in this context.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results on CWQ and WebQSP datasets provide strong evidence of the effectiveness of the proposed method.  Ablation studies are performed to identify key contributing factors.
    * **Efficiency:** The paper demonstrates that the approach can improve performance while reducing the number of LLM calls, making it more efficient than some competing methods.
    * **Comprehensive Experiments**: The experiments rigorously test the various parameters involved.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on LLMs for Decomposition:** The method depends on the quality of the sub-questions generated by the LLM. This introduces a potential source of error and limits the method's robustness.
    * **Textualized Graphs:**  Converting the graph to a textual representation might lose some of the structured information present in the graph. While a graph encoder is used, it might not fully capture the richness of the relationships.
    * **Limited Scope of Evaluation:**  While the CWQ and WebQSP datasets are well-known, the paper could benefit from evaluation on a broader range of knowledge-intensive QA benchmarks. A more detailed error analysis could reveal insights into failure modes.
    * **Lack of comparison to chain of thought prompting**: Chain of thought prompting is a very powerful technique that would be relevant for a comparison.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in knowledge-guided LLMs, particularly in the areas of query decomposition, graph retrieval, and structured reasoning. The techniques introduced could be adapted and extended to other knowledge-intensive tasks, such as question answering over different types of knowledge bases or document retrieval.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the above analysis, I assign a score of **7** to this paper. While the individual components of the approach (query decomposition, graph retrieval) are not entirely new, the combination of these techniques, along with the hybrid retrieval mechanism and demonstration of increased efficiency, provides significant novelty. The clear experimental results and potential for impact in the field of knowledge-guided LLMs justify this score. The main detractor is the reliance on LLMs for decomposition without clear validation of decomposition.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Delving Into the Psychology of Machines: Exploring the Structure of Self-Regulated Learning via LLM-Generated Survey Responses
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13384v1)
- **Authors**: Leonie V. D. E. Vogelsmeier, Eduardo Oliveira, Kamila Misiejuk, Sonsoles López-Pernas, Mohammed Saqr
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) offer the potential to simulate human-like responses and behaviors, creating new opportunities for psychological science. In the context of self-regulated learning (SRL), if LLMs can reliably simulate survey responses at scale and speed, they could be used to test intervention scenarios, refine theoretical models, augment sparse datasets, and represent hard-to-reach populations. However, the validity of LLM-generated survey responses remains uncertain, with limited research focused on SRL and existing studies beyond SRL yielding mixed results. Therefore, in this study, we examined LLM-generated responses to the 44-item Motivated Strategies for Learning Questionnaire (MSLQ; Pintrich \& De Groot, 1990), a widely used instrument assessing students' learning strategies and academic motivation. Particularly, we used the LLMs GPT-4o, Claude 3.7 Sonnet, Gemini 2 Flash, LLaMA 3.1-8B, and Mistral Large. We analyzed item distributions, the psychological network of the theoretical SRL dimensions, and psychometric validity based on the latent factor structure. Our results suggest that Gemini 2 Flash was the most promising LLM, showing considerable sampling variability and producing underlying dimensions and theoretical relationships that align with prior theory and empirical findings. At the same time, we observed discrepancies and limitations, underscoring both the potential and current constraints of using LLMs for simulating psychological survey data and applying it in educational contexts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the feasibility of using Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate synthetic survey responses for self-regulated learning (SRL) research. It examines the psychometric properties of LLM-generated data from five different LLMs (GPT-4o, Claude 3.7 Sonnet, Gemini 2 Flash, LLaMA 3.1-8B, and Mistral Large) in the context of the Motivated Strategies for Learning Questionnaire (MSLQ). The analysis focuses on item distributions, psychological network structure, and factor structure to assess how well LLMs can reproduce human-like responses and the underlying theoretical relationships within the SRL domain.  The findings suggest that Gemini 2 Flash performs best, exhibiting the most sampling variability and aligning with theoretical SRL dimensions. However, the authors also identify discrepancies and limitations, highlighting both the potential and current constraints of using LLMs for simulating psychological survey data in educational contexts. The paper concludes by discussing the opportunities (e.g., preliminary testing, intervention simulations) and threats (e.g., lack of internal beliefs, reproducibility issues, limited validity) associated with utilizing LLMs in SRL research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs for generating survey data, while not entirely new, has limited application in educational psychology, specifically in the SRL domain. Previous research primarily concentrated on political opinions, general personality traits, or specific skills assessments. The application to a complex psychological construct like SRL adds a degree of novelty. The systematic comparison of multiple LLMs using different analytical methods also contributes to the novelty.
*   **Significance:**  The study's significance lies in its exploration of a potentially powerful tool for educational research. If LLMs can reliably generate survey data, it opens avenues for rapid prototyping of interventions, testing theoretical models, and representing diverse populations. The paper's careful evaluation of the strengths and limitations provides valuable insights for researchers considering using LLMs in their work.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous methodology:**  The paper employs a range of analytical techniques including descriptive statistics, psychological network analysis, exploratory factor analysis (EFA), and confirmatory factor analysis (CFA).
    *   **Comprehensive comparison:** The evaluation involves five different LLMs, providing a broad overview of the capabilities and limitations of different models.
    *   **Balanced Discussion:** The discussion section is well-balanced, addressing both the potential benefits and the significant challenges associated with using LLMs for survey data generation. The limitations section is insightful and addresses some important caveats.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Lack of Human Comparison Data:** The absence of a human-completed dataset for comparison is a significant limitation. While the study refers to existing literature on MSLQ's psychometric properties, direct comparison to human data would strengthen the conclusions about the LLMs' ability to replicate real-world patterns.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Prompt Engineering:** The paper acknowledges that prompt sensitivity is a limitation but doesn't deeply explore alternative prompting strategies to improve the quality of LLM-generated data.
    *   **Generalizability:** As LLMs rapidly evolve, findings regarding a specific model may quickly become outdated. While the study compares several LLMs, it’s uncertain if findings from Gemini would still be top tier.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is a solid step towards assessing LLMs for survey data generation, but some points need further development. More empirical findings are required to support that theory and previous research align with LLM analysis.
*   **Rigorous Rationale:** Gemini has limited analysis results and lacks comparison data, yet seems to be the top LLM analyzed in this article. It appears that Gemini would be useful for preliminary testing in education research, and could be powerful given further development and comparison with other LLMs and datasets.

### Zero-Shot Solving of Imaging Inverse Problems via Noise-Refined Likelihood Guided Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13391v1)
- **Authors**: Zhen Wang, Hongyi Liu, Zhihui Wei
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have achieved remarkable success in imaging inverse problems owing to their powerful generative capabilities. However, existing approaches typically rely on models trained for specific degradation types, limiting their generalizability to various degradation scenarios. To address this limitation, we propose a zero-shot framework capable of handling various imaging inverse problems without model retraining. We introduce a likelihood-guided noise refinement mechanism that derives a closed-form approximation of the likelihood score, simplifying score estimation and avoiding expensive gradient computations. This estimated score is subsequently utilized to refine the model-predicted noise, thereby better aligning the restoration process with the generative framework of diffusion models. In addition, we integrate the Denoising Diffusion Implicit Models (DDIM) sampling strategy to further improve inference efficiency. The proposed mechanism can be applied to both optimization-based and sampling-based schemes, providing an effective and flexible zero-shot solution for imaging inverse problems. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method achieves superior performance across multiple inverse problems, particularly in compressive sensing, delivering high-quality reconstructions even at an extremely low sampling rate (5%).
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Zero-Shot Solving of Imaging Inverse Problems via Noise-Refined Likelihood Guided Diffusion Models."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a zero-shot framework for solving imaging inverse problems using diffusion models. Unlike existing methods that require task-specific training, this approach aims to handle various degradations without retraining. The core idea involves a "likelihood-guided noise refinement" mechanism. The paper derives a closed-form approximation of the likelihood score, which simplifies score estimation and avoids expensive gradient computations via backpropagation. This score is then used to refine the model-predicted noise during the diffusion process, aligning the restoration more closely with the generative framework. Furthermore, the method incorporates the DDIM sampling strategy for increased inference efficiency. The approach can be applied to both optimization-based and sampling-based schemes.  Experiments demonstrate superior performance across multiple inverse problems, particularly in compressive sensing at low sampling rates.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   *Strengths:* The most significant novelty is the closed-form approximation of the likelihood score and its use in refining the noise predicted by the diffusion model. Avoiding backpropagation for the likelihood score calculation is a significant computational improvement. The approach provides a more direct and efficient way to incorporate observational data into the diffusion process than existing methods. This is particularly attractive given that backpropagation is often the bottleneck in score estimation. The integration of the likelihood score to *refine the noise* rather than *manipulating the samples* is also a notable contribution. Finally, its applicability to both sampling-based and optimization-based inversion strategies offers flexibility.
    *   *Weaknesses:* While the closed-form approximation is valuable, the authors make an independence assumption (between the noise predictor output and the input variable) to simplify the computation of the Jacobian matrix, This introduces an approximation error, and its impact needs to be carefully considered. Also, similar approximations of the likelihood score exist, such as in ΠGDM, but without the detailed theoretical justification and noise refinement, as well as requiring explicit Jacobian computation, and the work should be differentiated. There is very little mathematical analysis of the sensitivity to the error created by the assumption.
    *   *Claimed Innovation:* Overall, the paper presents a significant innovation as the "noise refinement" guided by an efficient estimate of the likelihood score offers clear computational benefits while remaining within the generative framework of diffusion models. The efficiency is important here for practical application of these potentially computationally intensive methods.

*   **Significance:**

    *   *Strengths:* The zero-shot capability is highly significant.  It addresses a key limitation of diffusion models, which often require extensive training for specific degradation types. The demonstrated performance in compressive sensing, especially at very low sampling rates, highlights the practical importance of this work for applications where data acquisition is expensive or constrained. The algorithm only needing the adjustment of two parameters offers tremendous ease in deployment. The computational benefits, resulting from the avoidance of backpropagation, directly impacts the speed and scalability of diffusion-based image restoration.
    *   *Weaknesses:* While the experimental results are promising, it would be helpful to have a more extensive comparison against state-of-the-art zero-shot methods, particularly in terms of perceptual quality metrics. The reliance on specific pre-trained diffusion models could also be seen as a limitation, as the performance may depend on the quality and characteristics of the pre-trained model. As presented in Section 4.2, sometimes improvement between proposed method and others is rather small, which makes the impact not as significant as it should be. A more exhaustive comparison in Section 4.2.3 to other methods should be presented to highlight the key strengths over other methods.
    *   *Potential Impact:* The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by providing a practical and efficient zero-shot approach to solving a wide range of imaging inverse problems. The efficient and refined approximation of the likelihood is key to the success of the method, and could provide a template for future research in diffusion-based image restoration.

*   **Justification for score**

    The paper offers a valuable contribution by providing an efficient noise refinement method. However, the impact of the simplification (noise independence assumption) on performance requires deeper investigation. The gains compared to existing methods, although present, are not always significant. Also, a comparison of runtimes between the proposed method and other baselines should be presented. The theoretical formulation and derivation of the closed-form approximation, and its novel use in a refinement framework offer a solid foundation.
