### TimeMaster: Training Time-Series Multimodal LLMs to Reason via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13705v1)
- **Authors**: Junru Zhang, Lang Feng, Xu Guo, Yuhan Wu, Yabo Dong, Duanqing Xu
- **Abstract**: Time-series reasoning remains a significant challenge in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) due to the dynamic temporal patterns, ambiguous semantics, and lack of temporal priors. In this work, we introduce TimeMaster, a reinforcement learning (RL)-based method that enables time-series MLLMs to perform structured, interpretable reasoning directly over visualized time-series inputs and task prompts. TimeMaster adopts a three-part structured output format, reasoning, classification, and domain-specific extension, and is optimized via a composite reward function that aligns format adherence, prediction accuracy, and open-ended insight quality. The model is trained using a two-stage pipeline: we first apply supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to establish a good initialization, followed by Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) at the token level to enable stable and targeted reward-driven improvement in time-series reasoning. We evaluate TimeMaster on the TimerBed benchmark across six real-world classification tasks based on Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct. TimeMaster achieves state-of-the-art performance, outperforming both classical time-series models and few-shot GPT-4o by over 14.6% and 7.3% performance gain, respectively. Notably, TimeMaster goes beyond time-series classification: it also exhibits expert-like reasoning behavior, generates context-aware explanations, and delivers domain-aligned insights. Our results highlight that reward-driven RL can be a scalable and promising path toward integrating temporal understanding into time-series MLLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TimeMaster: Training Time-Series Multimodal LLMs to Reason via Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TimeMaster, a reinforcement learning (RL)-based method designed to enhance the reasoning capabilities of time-series multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Addressing the challenges of temporal pattern analysis, ambiguous semantics, and the lack of temporal priors in existing approaches, TimeMaster trains MLLMs to perform structured, interpretable reasoning over visualized time-series inputs and task prompts. The method utilizes a three-part structured output format (reasoning, classification, and domain-specific extension) and optimizes the model with a composite reward function. TimeMaster is trained via a two-stage pipeline: supervised fine-tuning (SFT) followed by Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) at the token level. The model is evaluated on the TimerBed benchmark, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance on various real-world classification tasks. The results highlight TimeMaster's expert-like reasoning behavior, generation of context-aware explanations, and delivery of domain-aligned insights, showcasing the potential of RL for integrating temporal understanding into time-series MLLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the application of reinforcement learning (RL) to explicitly train time-series MLLMs for structured reasoning and explainability. Previous work primarily relied on supervised fine-tuning or prompting techniques. The structured output format, and the composite reward function tailored to format adherence, accuracy and insight quality, contributes to the paper's innovative character.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work resides in its potential to unlock more interpretable and actionable insights from time-series data using MLLMs. The ability to generate explanations and domain-aligned suggestions moves beyond simple classification or forecasting, addressing a crucial need in real-world applications like healthcare and industrial monitoring. Empirical results indicate significant performance gains compared to SOTA methods (GPT-40 and classical time series models).
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The RL framework for training time-series MLLMs directly addresses the limitations of relying solely on static, supervised datasets.
    *   The structured output format and composite reward function promotes both accuracy and explainability.
    *   Empirical results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on the TimerBed benchmark, surpassing both traditional time-series models and existing MLLM approaches.
    *   The method is parameter-efficient, using a relatively small (3B) parameter model.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on GPT-40 for generating the initial supervised data (for SFT) introduces potential bias.
    *   The reward function is partially based on LLM-as-a-judge, which may be subjective or inconsistent. While prompting methodologies exist to reduce this, it's a inherent limitation.
    *   The paper mentions that in HAR dataset (human activity recognition), model lags slightly compared to classical methods because of sensor inter-variable data relations in that dataset, requiring fine-grained temporal reasoning supervision. The work addresses broad challenges.
    *   The paper acknowledges limitations in handling highly complex or ambiguous scenarios, suggesting that human oversight is still necessary.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the direction of time-series analysis by demonstrating the value of RL-based training for MLLMs. This work could encourage further research into more sophisticated reward structures, exploration strategies, and model architectures tailored to the specific challenges of temporal reasoning. By promoting interpretable and actionable insights, TimeMaster could also foster greater adoption of MLLMs in real-world applications.

**Score Justification:**

Considering the paper's strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**. While the use of RL for time-series MLLMs and the focus on structured reasoning represent significant innovations, the reliance on GPT-40 and LLM-as-a-judge introduces some level of subjectivity and dependency on external resources. It surpasses much of the standard SOTA work but doesn't completely resolve key issues in the application of the tech it leverages (LLMs) or potential bias.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Weakest Link in the Chain: Security Vulnerabilities in Advanced Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13726v1)
- **Authors**: Arjun Krishna, Aaditya Rastogi, Erick Galinkin
- **Abstract**: The introduction of advanced reasoning capabilities have improved the problem-solving performance of large language models, particularly on math and coding benchmarks. However, it remains unclear whether these reasoning models are more or less vulnerable to adversarial prompt attacks than their non-reasoning counterparts. In this work, we present a systematic evaluation of weaknesses in advanced reasoning models compared to similar non-reasoning models across a diverse set of prompt-based attack categories. Using experimental data, we find that on average the reasoning-augmented models are \emph{slightly more robust} than non-reasoning models (42.51\% vs 45.53\% attack success rate, lower is better). However, this overall trend masks significant category-specific differences: for certain attack types the reasoning models are substantially \emph{more vulnerable} (e.g., up to 32 percentage points worse on a tree-of-attacks prompt), while for others they are markedly \emph{more robust} (e.g., 29.8 points better on cross-site scripting injection). Our findings highlight the nuanced security implications of advanced reasoning in language models and emphasize the importance of stress-testing safety across diverse adversarial techniques.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score and a detailed justification.

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the security vulnerabilities of advanced reasoning language models (LLMs) compared to their non-reasoning counterparts. It systematically evaluates several model families across various prompt-based attack categories using the *garak* red-teaming framework. The study reveals a nuanced picture: while reasoning models exhibit slightly improved overall robustness, their performance varies significantly across different attack types. They are more vulnerable to attacks that leverage their reasoning capabilities, such as TAP and suffix injection, but more resilient against straightforward attacks like XSS and malware generation. The paper highlights specific model family differences, suggesting that the impact of reasoning on security depends on the model's architecture and alignment techniques. Ultimately, it argues that advanced reasoning introduces both hardening and weakening effects, necessitating comprehensive security evaluations tailored to each specific reasoning model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper provides a large-scale, systematic comparison of reasoning vs. non-reasoning models under adversarial prompts. This is a valuable contribution, as previous work has largely been anecdotal or limited in scope.
    *   **Comprehensive Attack Set:** The use of a diverse set of 35 probe variants covering 7 security-relevant categories strengthens the validity and generalizability of the findings. The use of the *garak* framework is a well-chosen tool for the evaluation.
    *   **Nuanced Findings:** The paper avoids oversimplification, revealing that reasoning doesn't uniformly improve or degrade security. Identifying specific attack categories where reasoning introduces new vulnerabilities is a key contribution.
    *   **Per-Model Family Analysis:** Breaking down results by model family (DeepSeek, Qwen, LLaMA) provides valuable insights into the specific architectural and training factors that influence security. It acknowledges the heterogeneity of LLMs and their reasoning mechanisms.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The paper offers practical implications for deploying reasoning models in AI agentic systems, emphasizing the need for tailored security measures. Highlighting areas where reasoning becomes a 'weak link' directs future research towards targeted mitigation strategies.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Model Set:** While three model families are evaluated, the choice is limited by public availability of base and reasoning variant models. A larger and more diverse set of LLMs would strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Attack Success Rate (ASR) as the Primary Metric:** Reliance on strict success criteria for ASR might understate certain vulnerabilities. Partial refusals or obfuscation, while not counted as successes, still represent potential security concerns.
    *   **Lack of Causal Explanation:** The paper identifies correlations between reasoning and vulnerability but doesn't fully explain the underlying causal mechanisms. Further research is needed to understand *why* reasoning models are more susceptible to TAP and suffix injection attacks. Simply stating that certain models "over-emphasize the input" provides only a high-level explanation.
    *   **Generalization to Future Models:** The field of LLMs is rapidly evolving. While the systematic approach is valuable, the specific vulnerabilities identified might become less relevant as models improve. The core insights about reasoning trade-offs, however, will likely remain pertinent.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper makes a novel contribution by providing a rigorous empirical assessment of the security implications of advanced reasoning in LLMs. It goes beyond anecdotal evidence and performs a systematic comparison, revealing specific vulnerabilities that were previously under-explored. The identification of "weakest link" attack categories is particularly valuable for focusing future security research.
    While the study is limited by the available set of models and the use of ASR as the primary metric, it lays a strong foundation for further investigation into the security of reasoning-enhanced LLMs. It's also timely, given the increasing integration of these models into real-world applications.

**Overall Score:**

Given the systematic approach, nuanced findings, and actionable insights, but acknowledging the limitations of model set and causal explanations, I assign the following score:

Score: 7.5

**Justification:**
The score is justified by the paper's rigorous methodology and contribution to the emerging area of LLM security, specifically concerning reasoning models. Its contribution moves beyond anecdotal evidence, providing a robust framework to comparatively analyze reasoning and non-reasoning language models for security vulnerabilities using an industry-recognized, red-teaming tool. It provides concrete insights and informs future research directions. While it has limitations in the scope of models used and a need for greater causal elucidation of identified vulnerabilities, it sets a standard for quantitative evaluation in this domain. If a deeper theoretical explanation of causality had been provided, along with the expanded model set, the score would have been higher, but, as it stands, it provides a significant and carefully presented empirical analysis.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Attribution-guided Pruning for Compression, Circuit Discovery, and Targeted Correction in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13727v1)
- **Authors**: Sayed Mohammad Vakilzadeh Hatefi, Maximilian Dreyer, Reduan Achtibat, Patrick Kahardipraja, Thomas Wiegand, Wojciech Samek, Sebastian Lapuschkin
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are central to many contemporary AI applications, yet their extensive parameter counts pose significant challenges for deployment in memory- and compute-constrained environments. Recent works in eXplainable AI (XAI), particularly on attribution methods, suggest that interpretability can also enable model compression by identifying and removing components irrelevant to inference. In this paper, we leverage Layer-wise Relevance Propagation (LRP) to perform attribution-guided pruning of LLMs. While LRP has shown promise in structured pruning for vision models, we extend it to unstructured pruning in LLMs and demonstrate that it can substantially reduce model size with minimal performance loss. Our method is especially effective in extracting task-relevant subgraphs -- so-called ``circuits'' -- which can represent core functions (e.g., indirect object identification). Building on this, we introduce a technique for model correction, by selectively removing circuits responsible for spurious behaviors (e.g., toxic outputs). All in all, we gather these techniques as a uniform holistic framework and showcase its effectiveness and limitations through extensive experiments for compression, circuit discovery and model correction on Llama and OPT models, highlighting its potential for improving both model efficiency and safety. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/erfanhatefi/SparC3.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a unified framework (SparC3) for attribution-guided pruning of Large Language Models (LLMs). It leverages Layer-wise Relevance Propagation (LRP) to identify and prune parameters based on their relevance to either general tasks, specific tasks (for circuit discovery), or undesirable behaviors (for model correction). The framework is demonstrated across three applications: compressing models for efficiency, discovering task-specific circuits or subgraphs representing key functionalities (e.g., indirect object identification), and correcting models by removing components responsible for toxic outputs or repetitive text generation. The experiments are conducted on Llama and OPT models, showing effective compression with minimal performance loss, identification of task-relevant subgraphs, and successful suppression of undesirable behaviors. The authors also compare against methods like Wanda [31], showing situations where LRP-based pruning is more effective.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in unifying attribution-guided pruning for compression, circuit discovery, and model correction in a single framework tailored for LLMs. While LRP-based structured pruning has been explored in vision models, its extension to unstructured pruning in LLMs, along with the circuit discovery and model correction applications, represents a significant contribution. Further, the comparison against Wanda and its findings highlight situations and specific layers where LRP is superior represent a meaningful addition to the existing knowledge. Prior works address model compression and toxicity separately, however, using a framework centered on attribution guided pruning to combine these goals is novel.
*   **Significance:** The work addresses critical challenges in LLM deployment: resource constraints and unwanted behaviors. Compression, circuit discovery, and model correction are all important areas, and the paper provides a practical approach to tackle them with the use of attribution based pruning. The ability to identify and remove task-specific circuits or suppress harmful behaviors without extensive retraining offers a valuable tool for LLM developers and practitioners. The demonstration of toxicity reduction using LRP is important for improving LLM safety.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The unified framework simplifies the application of attribution-guided pruning to different tasks.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The experiments on Llama and OPT models across various tasks provides solid evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Comparison to Baselines:** The comparison against Wanda highlights the advantages of LRP in certain scenarios.
    *   **Practical Application:** The model correction part, in particular the toxicity mitigation using the proposed approach is of great practical importance.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Scalability:** While the approach is demonstrated on Llama and OPT models, the paper does not address the scalability of LRP to larger, state-of-the-art LLMs.  LRP can be computationally intensive.
    *   **Reference Set Dependence:** The effectiveness of the method relies heavily on the quality of the reference samples. The creation of those sample sets is not a part of the automated process. Developing more automated and robust methods for generating suitable reference sets would be valuable.
    *   **Granularity limits:** The paper's analysis of where to perform the model correction or circuit discovery only touches on the layers to target and pruning is only unstructured or strutured, however a more detailed analysis and framework for finding the optimal configurations is still required.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more efficient, interpretable, and safer LLMs. The unified framework and the demonstration of its effectiveness can encourage further research in attribution-guided model editing and compression techniques. Further analysis for identifying optimal configuration to perform the task can be followed by the research community.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a well-executed study with a unified framework addressing significant challenges in the field of LLMs, and presents valuable findings for when the proposed methodology is a good fit for the target task. While LRP-based pruning is not entirely novel, its application to LLMs with unified framework with practical benefits, especially in circuit discovery and model correction, represents a valuable contribution. The paper also acknowledges its limitations. Overall, the paper showcases significant novelty and potential for practical impact.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Instruction Following by Boosting Attention of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13734v1)
- **Authors**: Vitoria Guardieiro, Adam Stein, Avishree Khare, Eric Wong
- **Abstract**: Controlling the generation of large language models (LLMs) remains a central challenge to ensure their safe and reliable deployment. While prompt engineering and finetuning are common approaches, recent work has explored latent steering, a lightweight technique that alters LLM internal activations to guide generation. However, subsequent studies revealed latent steering's effectiveness to be limited, often underperforming simple instruction prompting. To address this limitation, we first establish a benchmark across diverse behaviors for standardized evaluation of steering techniques. Building on insights from this benchmark, we introduce Instruction Attention Boosting (InstABoost), a latent steering method that boosts the strength of instruction prompting by altering the model's attention during generation. InstABoost combines the strengths of existing approaches and is theoretically supported by prior work that suggests that in-context rule following in transformer-based models can be controlled by manipulating attention on instructions. Empirically, InstABoost demonstrates superior control success compared to both traditional prompting and latent steering.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of controlling the behavior of Large Language Models (LLMs) through steering techniques. It identifies limitations of existing latent steering methods, which often underperform simple instruction prompting. To overcome this, the authors introduce "Instruction Attention Boosting" (INSTABOOST), a novel latent steering method that amplifies the attention given to instructions during generation. They show that INSTABOOST combines the strengths of existing approaches, is theoretically supported, and empirically demonstrates superior control compared to both traditional prompting and other latent steering methods across a standardized benchmark of tasks. The paper also demonstrates that INSTABOOST maintains high generation fluency compared to other methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach (INSTABOOST) to latent steering. While the idea of manipulating attention is not entirely new, the specific application to boost instruction following is a well-motivated contribution. The theoretical basis for the method, linking attention manipulation to rule-following, adds to the novelty.
*   **Significance:** The research addresses a critical problem: ensuring the safe and reliable deployment of LLMs. The standardized benchmark and comparative analysis of different steering techniques are significant contributions, clarifying their relative strengths and weaknesses. INSTABOOST's ability to outperform existing methods, maintain fluency, and effectively remove unwanted alignments has practical implications for improving LLM control.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear motivation.
    *   Strong theoretical grounding.
    *   Rigorous experimental evaluation with a standardized benchmark.
    *   Demonstrated superior performance compared to baselines.
    *   Analysis of the trade-off between accuracy and fluency, highlighting INSTABOOST's advantage.
    *   Demonstrates the ability to not only steer towards desired behaviors, but also remove unintended alignments (e.g., from safety fine-tuning).
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method is evaluated only with Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct models. It would be stronger with evaluations on more models (i.e. GPT/Gemini family, larger-scale models).
    *   The evaluation tasks are limited to a few common steering scenarios (emotion, toxicity, etc.). More diverse and complex tasks would further validate the method's robustness.
    *   The approach may not work well for tasks needing longer instructions or instructions which the model has not seen during training. This is acknowledged in the limitations section, but further research exploring this is necessary.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the field of LLM control and safety. INSTABOOST provides a promising new direction for latent steering, and the standardized benchmark can serve as a valuable resource for future research. The findings will encourage further exploration of attention manipulation techniques and a more systematic evaluation of steering methods.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by tackling the limitations of existing latent steering methods. INSTABOOST's novel approach, strong performance, and practical implications warrant a high score. While there are some limitations, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses, making it a significant advance in LLM control.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Evaluating Large Language Models for Phishing Detection, Self-Consistency, Faithfulness, and Explainability
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13746v1)
- **Authors**: Shova Kuikel, Aritran Piplai, Palvi Aggarwal
- **Abstract**: Phishing attacks remain one of the most prevalent and persistent cybersecurity threat with attackers continuously evolving and intensifying tactics to evade the general detection system. Despite significant advances in artificial intelligence and machine learning, faithfully reproducing the interpretable reasoning with classification and explainability that underpin phishing judgments remains challenging. Due to recent advancement in Natural Language Processing, Large Language Models (LLMs) show a promising direction and potential for improving domain specific phishing classification tasks. However, enhancing the reliability and robustness of classification models requires not only accurate predictions from LLMs but also consistent and trustworthy explanations aligning with those predictions. Therefore, a key question remains: can LLMs not only classify phishing emails accurately but also generate explanations that are reliably aligned with their predictions and internally self-consistent? To answer these questions, we have fine-tuned transformer based models, including BERT, Llama models, and Wizard, to improve domain relevance and make them more tailored to phishing specific distinctions, using Binary Sequence Classification, Contrastive Learning (CL) and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). To that end, we examined their performance in phishing classification and explainability by applying the ConsistenCy measure based on SHAPley values (CC SHAP), which measures prediction explanation token alignment to test the model's internal faithfulness and consistency and uncover the rationale behind its predictions and reasoning. Overall, our findings show that Llama models exhibit stronger prediction explanation token alignment with higher CC SHAP scores despite lacking reliable decision making accuracy, whereas Wizard achieves better prediction accuracy but lower CC SHAP scores.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, focusing on novelty, significance, strengths, weaknesses, and a justified score.

**Summary:**

The paper "Evaluating Large Language Models for Phishing Detection, Self-Consistency, Faithfulness, and Explainability" investigates the performance of various Large Language Models (LLMs) in the context of phishing email detection. It examines not only the accuracy of these models in classifying emails as phishing or legitimate ("ham") but also their ability to provide internally consistent and trustworthy explanations for their classifications.  The authors fine-tune models such as BERT, Llama-2-7B, Llama-3-8B and Wizard-7B using binary sequence classification, direct preference optimization (DPO), and contrastive learning. They then use a metric called ConsistenCy measure based on SHAPley values (CC-SHAP) to evaluate the alignment between the models' predictions and their explanations, measuring their internal faithfulness and consistency. The key finding is a trade-off: some models (like Llama) exhibit higher CC-SHAP scores but lower accuracy, while others (like Wizard) achieve better accuracy but lower CC-SHAP scores. This suggests that high self-consistency in explanation does not guarantee higher accuracy in phishing detection. The paper highlights the challenges of balancing human-like uncertainty with reliability in LLMs for cybersecurity applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its systematic evaluation of LLMs' explainability and self-consistency, specifically within the phishing detection domain. While LLMs have been applied to cybersecurity tasks before, the focus on the *alignment* between predictions and explanations using CC-SHAP is a valuable contribution. The investigation into the performance of different fine-tuning methods contributes as well. However, applying existing evaluation metrics such as CC-SHAP to LLMs in this setting may not be ground breaking novelty.

*   **Significance:** Phishing remains a significant cybersecurity threat, and the development of effective detection methods is crucial. The paper's investigation into the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in this area is therefore highly relevant. The finding that high explanation consistency doesn't necessarily correlate with higher accuracy is important, as it challenges assumptions about the trustworthiness of LLM-based security solutions. The insights on human-like uncertainty vs. reliability are also significant from the perspective of security-focused AI design.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates multiple LLM architectures and fine-tuning techniques.
    *   **Focus on Explainability:**  It goes beyond simply measuring accuracy and delves into the models' reasoning processes.
    *   **Use of CC-SHAP:** This provides a quantifiable measure of prediction-explanation alignment.
    *   **Clear Presentation of Results:** The results are presented clearly, with tables and figures to support the findings.
    *   **Real-World Relevance:** Addresses a practical and pressing cybersecurity problem.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset:** Although the dataset is reasonable, it is not particularly large, which could limit the generalizability of the findings. (5000 emails split for various trainings)
    *   **CC-SHAP Limitations:** While CC-SHAP is a useful metric, it is still a relatively simple measure of explanation quality. More sophisticated methods might be needed to capture the full complexity of LLM reasoning.
    *   **Domain Expertise Gap in Explanations?:** The "explanations" generated by LLMs are often summaries or interpretations of features rather than deeper insights into phishing tactics that an experienced security analyst would use. The paper would benefit from comparing these "explanations" against those of human experts.
    *   **Lack of Comparative Human Evaluation:** The paper focuses on internal consistency but lacks a direct comparison of LLMs' explanations with human judgment. This omission limits the assessment of the *external* validity or usefulness of the explanations.
    *   **Performance on DPO:** Poor performance of DPO fine-tuning across all models could be down to hyper-parameter optimization which is not addressed well in the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper could influence future research by highlighting the importance of explainability and self-consistency in LLM-based security systems. It could also prompt the development of more sophisticated evaluation metrics and fine-tuning techniques that balance accuracy with trustworthiness. Further, it should encourage more research on techniques to improve alignment and prevent false positives/negatives.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by systematically investigating the explainability and self-consistency of LLMs in the context of phishing detection. However, it's not a game-changing breakthrough. The limitations in dataset size, reliance on CC-SHAP, and lack of comparative human evaluation prevent it from achieving a higher score. The core idea of analyzing explanation alignment is strong, but the execution has room for improvement. Overall, the paper has good conceptual strength, decent experimental scope, and is well-written.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Steering LLM Thinking with Budget Guidance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13752v1)
- **Authors**: Junyan Li, Wenshuo Zhao, Yang Zhang, Chuang Gan
- **Abstract**: Recent deep-thinking large language models often reason extensively to improve performance, but such lengthy reasoning is not always desirable, as it incurs excessive inference costs with disproportionate performance gains. Controlling reasoning length without sacrificing performance is therefore important, but remains challenging, especially under tight thinking budgets. We propose budget guidance, a simple yet effective method for steering the reasoning process of LLMs toward a target budget without requiring any LLM fine-tuning. Our approach introduces a lightweight predictor that models a Gamma distribution over the remaining thinking length during next-token generation. This signal is then used to guide generation in a soft, token-level manner, ensuring that the overall reasoning trace adheres to the specified thinking budget. Budget guidance enables natural control of the thinking length, along with significant token efficiency improvements over baseline methods on challenging math benchmarks. For instance, it achieves up to a 26% accuracy gain on the MATH-500 benchmark under tight budgets compared to baseline methods, while maintaining competitive accuracy with only 63% of the thinking tokens used by the full-thinking model. Budget guidance also generalizes to broader task domains and exhibits emergent capabilities, such as estimating question difficulty. The source code is available at: https://github.com/UMass-Embodied-AGI/BudgetGuidance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces "Budget Guidance," a novel approach to control the reasoning length of Large Language Models (LLMs) at inference time, without requiring fine-tuning. The method trains a lightweight auxiliary predictor to estimate the remaining reasoning length at each token generation step. This prediction is then used to modulate the LLM's generation probability, guiding the reasoning process towards a target budget of tokens. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their method on math reasoning benchmarks, showing improved token efficiency and a favorable trade-off between thinking length and accuracy compared to baseline methods like budget forcing and no-thinking baselines. The paper also investigates the method's generalizability to other tasks and its ability to estimate task difficulty.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using a predictor to guide the reasoning length of LLMs is relatively novel. While methods exist for shortening or pruning reasoning, and others enforce hard limits, Budget Guidance stands out by attempting a more principled and flexible control mechanism that doesn't abruptly truncate the reasoning process. The use of a Gamma distribution to model remaining reasoning length is also a somewhat novel aspect, though using auxiliary predictors to guide generation draws inspiration from the diffusion model literature.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important practical problem: the excessive computational cost and latency of very long reasoning chains in LLMs. By providing a mechanism to control reasoning length without significant performance degradation, Budget Guidance could contribute to making deep-thinking LLMs more efficient and deployable in resource-constrained environments. The finding that the method generalizes across datasets and tasks is a valuable contribution as well. The emergence of the predictor being able to estimate problem difficulty hints at interesting emergent capabilities that warrant further exploration.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong empirical results:** The paper presents compelling evidence of the method's effectiveness on multiple benchmarks, demonstrating significant token efficiency gains and improved accuracy compared to budget forcing.
    *   **No fine-tuning required:** A key strength is the method's ability to control reasoning length without requiring LLM fine-tuning, making it more accessible and easier to integrate into existing inference pipelines.
    *   **Generalizability:** The demonstration of out-of-domain transferability suggests that the predictor can learn generalizable patterns about reasoning length and task difficulty.
    *   **Controlled Experiments:** Varying budget lengths to understand token efficiency.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited task diversity:** While the method shows promising results on math reasoning benchmarks, the number of tasks (4) and datasets (19) used is somewhat limited, so more rigorous testing of generalization is needed across other modalities.
    *   **Reliance on BERT:** The method uses BERT to build the predictor and while not fine-tuning a large language model, it does rely on another pretrained model.
    *   **Incremental improvement:** Budget Guidance seems to primarily provide a way to tune between performance and cost by cutting down on the number of tokens used. The method should be seen as an incremental improvement to budget forcing, rather than anything truly revolutionary.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the method proves to be robust and generalizable across a wider range of tasks and LLMs, it could become a valuable tool for optimizing the deployment and utilization of deep-thinking LLMs. It could also inspire further research into techniques for controlling and understanding the reasoning processes of these models.

**Justification:**

The method offers a promising approach to a significant problem, and the empirical results are convincing. However, the limited scope of the evaluation and the reliance on BERT suggest that there's still room for improvement and further investigation. The novelty lies more in the specific combination of techniques and the goal of flexible, fine-grained control rather than a fundamentally new theoretical insight.

### VideoPDE: Unified Generative PDE Solving via Video Inpainting Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13754v1)
- **Authors**: Edward Li, Zichen Wang, Jiahe Huang, Jeong Joon Park
- **Abstract**: We present a unified framework for solving partial differential equations (PDEs) using video-inpainting diffusion transformer models. Unlike existing methods that devise specialized strategies for either forward or inverse problems under full or partial observation, our approach unifies these tasks under a single, flexible generative framework. Specifically, we recast PDE-solving as a generalized inpainting problem, e.g., treating forward prediction as inferring missing spatiotemporal information of future states from initial conditions. To this end, we design a transformer-based architecture that conditions on arbitrary patterns of known data to infer missing values across time and space. Our method proposes pixel-space video diffusion models for fine-grained, high-fidelity inpainting and conditioning, while enhancing computational efficiency through hierarchical modeling. Extensive experiments show that our video inpainting-based diffusion model offers an accurate and versatile solution across a wide range of PDEs and problem setups, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "VideoPDE: Unified Generative PDE Solving via Video Inpainting Diffusion Models" introduces a novel framework, VideoPDE, for solving partial differential equations (PDEs).  Instead of relying on specialized methods for forward or inverse problems with varying degrees of observation, VideoPDE frames PDE solving as a video inpainting task. This allows the use of a single, flexible generative model based on a video diffusion transformer.  The core idea is to view forward simulation as predicting missing frames given initial conditions, and inverse problems as reconstructing past states from later observations.  The paper proposes a hierarchical, pixel-level video diffusion transformer (HV-DiT) that can condition on arbitrary spatiotemporal patterns of known data to infer missing values.  Experiments across a range of PDEs demonstrate that VideoPDE achieves state-of-the-art performance, even with minimal continuous measurements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of reframing PDE solving as video inpainting is a significant and clever shift in perspective.  Previous methods typically treated forward and inverse problems, and complete vs. partial observations, as separate challenges requiring different strategies. Unifying these under a generative video inpainting framework is a fresh and promising approach. The architectural contributions, particularly the hierarchical pixel-level diffusion transformer designed for this task, are also noteworthy, enabling better conditioning and efficiency compared to latent-space VDMs. The idea of encoding PDE solutions into video form is not entirely new, but the approach of using video inpainting is novel and non-trivial.

* **Significance:** The significance stems from several factors. First, unifying different PDE solving scenarios simplifies the development and deployment of neural PDE solvers. Second, the demonstrated performance, especially the ability to handle sparse data accurately and efficiently, opens possibilities for real-world applications where complete data is rare or expensive to acquire. Finally, the reliance on a generative model allows for non-deterministic solutions suitable for chaotic systems, which is a valuable characteristic. However, a potential limitation is the reliance on pixel-wise loss, which, the authors acknowledge, can penalize plausible reconstructions in chaotic systems when a blurry average would be more appropriate. A move to more sophisticated generative evaluation metrics could further improve the approach and results, especially for long-term predictions. Furthermore, despite claiming superior performance on "sparse measurement configurations," there are no experiments that specifically change sensor locations over time. This is arguably more reflective of "real-world scenarios."

* **Strengths:**
    * **Unified framework:**  A single model can handle forward, inverse, and partial observation scenarios.
    * **State-of-the-art performance:** Demonstrated across diverse PDEs and problem setups.
    * **Efficiency:** Hierarchical modeling improves computational efficiency.
    * **Handles sparse data:**  Accurate predictions with minimal measurements.
    * **Generative approach:** Capable of modeling non-deterministic systems.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Pixel-wise loss limitation:** Could be improved with distribution-based evaluation for chaotic systems.
    * **No temporal sensor data experiments:** Would enhance real-world applicability claims.
    * **Ablation studies could be stronger:** More analysis of design choices like pixel-space diffusion versus latent space would strengthen the paper.

* **Potential Influence:** VideoPDE has the potential to influence the development of neural PDE solvers, particularly for applications involving incomplete data or chaotic systems. The video inpainting framing provides a promising direction for unifying various PDE solving tasks and improving their accessibility. Other researchers in the field will likely build upon this approach by exploring different generative architectures, evaluation metrics, and extensions to 3D and high dimensional settings.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of neural PDE solvers. The novelty of framing PDE solving as video inpainting is significant, and the experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach. While some weaknesses exist, notably the limitations of pixel-wise loss and the lack of variable sensor configurations, the paper's strengths outweigh its limitations.

### AutoVLA: A Vision-Language-Action Model for End-to-End Autonomous Driving with Adaptive Reasoning and Reinforcement Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13757v1)
- **Authors**: Zewei Zhou, Tianhui Cai, Seth Z. Zhao, Yun Zhang, Zhiyu Huang, Bolei Zhou, Jiaqi Ma
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have shown promise for end-to-end autonomous driving by leveraging world knowledge and reasoning capabilities. However, current VLA models often struggle with physically infeasible action outputs, complex model structures, or unnecessarily long reasoning. In this paper, we propose AutoVLA, a novel VLA model that unifies reasoning and action generation within a single autoregressive generation model for end-to-end autonomous driving. AutoVLA performs semantic reasoning and trajectory planning directly from raw visual inputs and language instructions. We tokenize continuous trajectories into discrete, feasible actions, enabling direct integration into the language model. For training, we employ supervised fine-tuning to equip the model with dual thinking modes: fast thinking (trajectory-only) and slow thinking (enhanced with chain-of-thought reasoning). To further enhance planning performance and efficiency, we introduce a reinforcement fine-tuning method based on Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), reducing unnecessary reasoning in straightforward scenarios. Extensive experiments across real-world and simulated datasets and benchmarks, including nuPlan, nuScenes, Waymo, and CARLA, demonstrate the competitive performance of AutoVLA in both open-loop and closed-loop settings. Qualitative results showcase the adaptive reasoning and accurate planning capabilities of AutoVLA in diverse scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AutoVLA: A Vision-Language-Action Model for End-to-End Autonomous Driving with Adaptive Reasoning and Reinforcement Fine-Tuning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AutoVLA, a novel end-to-end autonomous driving framework based on Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models. AutoVLA unifies reasoning and action generation within a single autoregressive Transformer model, enabling adaptive switching between fast thinking (trajectory-only) and slow thinking (enhanced chain-of-thought reasoning) modes. The model discretizes continuous trajectories into feasible action tokens, integrating them directly into the language model. It employs supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to equip the model with dual-process capabilities and utilizes a reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) method based on Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to enhance planning performance and efficiency.  The paper presents extensive experiments across real-world and simulated datasets (nuPlan, nuScenes, Waymo, CARLA) demonstrating AutoVLA's competitive performance and adaptive reasoning capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects:
    *   **Unified Architecture:** The unification of reasoning and action generation into a single autoregressive model is a notable design choice. While others have explored VLA models for driving, the seamless integration is a good step.
    *   **Action Tokenization:** Discretizing trajectories into feasible action tokens and incorporating them into the VLM allows for more direct control over action outputs and ensures physical feasibility.
    *   **Adaptive Reasoning with GRPO:** The use of GRPO-based RFT to enable adaptive reasoning (fast vs. slow thinking) by minimizing unnecessary reasoning and improving efficiency is a key contribution.
    *   **Reasoning Data Annotation:** The design of an automated reasoning annotation pipeline using a large VLM (Qwen2.5-VL-72B) to generate high-quality chain-of-thought (CoT) annotations.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Improved Performance:** The paper demonstrates strong experimental results on various benchmarks, indicating that AutoVLA achieves competitive performance compared to existing state-of-the-art methods in both open-loop and closed-loop settings.
    *   **Enhanced Efficiency:** The RFT method significantly reduces runtime by minimizing redundant reasoning, which is crucial for real-time autonomous driving applications.
    *   **Adaptive Reasoning:** The adaptive reasoning capability allows the model to handle diverse driving scenarios more effectively, balancing planning accuracy and efficiency.
    *   **End-to-End Approach:** Bypassing the conventional planning layers, and moving directly with Action tokenization is good, since the errors can be accumulated across the modules.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear presentation of the proposed approach.
    *   Strong experimental validation across diverse datasets and benchmarks.
    *   Detailed ablation studies to demonstrate the effectiveness of individual components.
    *   Qualitative results showcasing the adaptive reasoning and planning capabilities of AutoVLA.
    *   The idea is well-motivated and the experiments are comprehensive.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The computational cost and GPU dependence of the model, particularly during inference, are limitations for real-time deployment. The paper mentions future work will focus on optimizing runtime efficiency and reducing computation overhead, but this is a critical concern.
    *   While the automated reasoning annotation pipeline is a valuable contribution, the quality and diversity of the generated CoT data may still be limited compared to human-annotated data. Further analysis on the limitations of this automated approach would be valuable.
    *   The RFT stage relies on specific reward functions, and the performance of AutoVLA may be sensitive to the design of these reward functions. A discussion on the challenges and limitations of reward shaping in this context would be beneficial.
    *   While the paper emphasizes the modularity improvements of the approach, the impact of the complexity of reasoning is not analyzed properly.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The proposed AutoVLA framework could serve as a promising direction for future research in end-to-end autonomous driving.
    *   The adaptive reasoning approach based on GRPO could be adopted and extended by other researchers to improve the efficiency and robustness of VLA models.
    *   The action tokenization method could be applied to other robotics tasks involving continuous action spaces.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

The paper makes a **significant** contribution to the field of end-to-end autonomous driving. The unification of reasoning and action generation, the action tokenization method, and the adaptive reasoning framework based on GRPO are all novel and well-motivated. The strong experimental results across multiple benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach. While the computational cost and potential limitations of the automated reasoning pipeline are weaknesses, the paper's strengths and potential influence outweigh these drawbacks. The score reflects that, although there is room to improve and refine, the contribution is valuable.

### Diagnosing and Improving Diffusion Models by Estimating the Optimal Loss Value
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13763v1)
- **Authors**: Yixian Xu, Shengjie Luo, Liwei Wang, Di He, Chang Liu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have achieved remarkable success in generative modeling. Despite more stable training, the loss of diffusion models is not indicative of absolute data-fitting quality, since its optimal value is typically not zero but unknown, leading to confusion between large optimal loss and insufficient model capacity. In this work, we advocate the need to estimate the optimal loss value for diagnosing and improving diffusion models. We first derive the optimal loss in closed form under a unified formulation of diffusion models, and develop effective estimators for it, including a stochastic variant scalable to large datasets with proper control of variance and bias. With this tool, we unlock the inherent metric for diagnosing the training quality of mainstream diffusion model variants, and develop a more performant training schedule based on the optimal loss. Moreover, using models with 120M to 1.5B parameters, we find that the power law is better demonstrated after subtracting the optimal loss from the actual training loss, suggesting a more principled setting for investigating the scaling law for diffusion models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem that diffusion model losses don't provide an absolute measure of data-fitting quality because their optimal values are non-zero and unknown. This makes it difficult to diagnose training quality, design training schedules, and analyze scaling laws. The authors derive analytical expressions for the optimal loss at each diffusion step, develop scalable estimators for it (including a variance-controlled, bias-balanced stochastic estimator for large datasets), and then use these estimators to improve diffusion model training. Specifically, they:

1.  Derive the analytical expression of the optimal loss at each diffusion step.
2.  Design scalable estimators for the optimal loss.
3.  Analyze the gap between actual loss and estimated optimal loss to diagnose underfitting regions across diffusion steps.
4.  Design a new training schedule based on this loss gap, leading to improved generation performance.
5.  Re-evaluate the scaling law of diffusion models using the loss gap as the data-fitting measure, finding a better fit to the power law.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in recognizing and addressing the limitations of using raw diffusion loss as a metric for model quality. Deriving the analytical optimal loss and designing effective estimators, particularly the scalable stochastic estimator, are significant technical contributions. Applying this framework to improve training schedules and re-evaluate scaling laws is also innovative. The paper is the first to introduce a computationally scalable method to estimate this value for any diffusion model.
*   **Significance:** The paper is of high significance because it provides a principled way to diagnose and improve diffusion models. The ability to estimate the optimal loss is a crucial tool for understanding model behavior, guiding training, and ensuring efficient scaling. The improvements in FID achieved by the new training schedule are substantial. The modification of the scaling law analysis offers a more accurate interpretation of the relationship between model size and performance. The code release will enable broad adoption by researchers and practitioners.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Theoretical Rigor:** The analytical derivation of optimal loss is sound and provides valuable insights.
    *   **Practical Scalability:** The proposed stochastic estimator is scalable to large datasets, making it widely applicable.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The improvements in generation performance and the refined scaling law analysis are convincingly demonstrated through experiments.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the problem, the proposed solution, and the results.
    *   **Impact:** The tool to measure the actual performance of a diffusion model compared to a hypothetical ideal diffusion model is important for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Overhead:** While the stochastic estimator is scalable, it still introduces computational overhead compared to simply using the raw loss. This could be a barrier to adoption in some resource-constrained settings.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The stochastic estimator involves hyperparameters (L, C) which require tuning. While the paper provides guidelines, optimal settings could depend on the specific dataset and model.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to become a standard reference for diagnosing and improving diffusion models. The optimal loss estimator and the loss gap-based training schedule could be widely adopted by researchers and practitioners. The refined scaling law analysis could also inform future model design and resource allocation strategies.

**Justification for the Score:**

While the computational overhead and hyperparameter sensitivity are minor drawbacks, the paper's strengths significantly outweigh its weaknesses. It tackles a fundamental problem in diffusion model analysis, provides a practical and theoretically sound solution, and demonstrates substantial improvements in model performance. This combination of theoretical rigor, practical scalability, and empirical validation warrants a high score.
